# Consideration of funding proposals – Addendum XI

## Funding proposal package for FP151

### Summary

This addendum contains the following six parts:

a) A funding proposal itinerary titled "Global Sub-national Climate Fund (ScF Global)" submitted by the International Union for Conservation of Nature (IUCN);

b) No-rejection letters issued by the national designated authority or focal point(s);

c) Environmental and social impact assessment;

d) Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment; and

e) Responses from the accredited entity to the independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment, and

f) Gender documentation of the funding proposal.

These documents are being distributed on a limited distribution basis only to Board Members and Alternate Board Members to ensure confidentiality of certain proprietary, legally privileged or commercially sensitive information of the entity.

The Paris Agreement, in which signatory countries pledge to keep global warming "well below" 2°C relative to pre-industrial levels and ideally to no more than 1.5°C, is an international political recognition of the urgent need for action on climate change. It represents a new approach to addressing climate change by acting on climate risk and ties closely to sustainable economic transition, representing an investment opportunity of last resort. The Paris Agreement also sets out a framework for countries to achieve their Nationally Determined Contributions (NDCs) under its Article 6, which allows for the creation of international cooperation mechanisms that can help Parties meet their NDCs. Impact Investing from the private sector is needed.

Subnational authorities have local responsibilities and local power. Indeed, decisions about renewable energy investments are often made at this level. These include decisions on land use, water allocation related to water and sanitation; restorative agriculture/aquaculture with the potential of adding nature based solutions (NBS) to climate change mitigation and adaptation strategies. The private sector can contribute with adaptation as co-benefits. It is also locally that the social and environmental impacts of policies and programs are most visible, and where they have their greatest impact on employment, stimulate the economy, and from the investor point of view, manage risk.

Although well-planned, even infrastructure projects that reduce greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions can also negatively affect biodiversity. For example, hydropower dams can lead to reduced water flow, which can cause Annex 22 for illustrations on negative impacts of renewable energy on biodiversity), as well as significant habitat loss and fragmentation. The same is true for wind farms, where the placement of projects need to be assessed in regard to reduced GHG emissions, rigorous safeguards, and ensure that the project will not have a negative impact on local communities or the environment. Ecosystem resilience, and the resilience of the infrastructure itself, in the face of climate change impacts. Ecosystem integrity must be maintained for climate resilience to be ensured.

The Green Climate Fund (GCF) is an international financial mechanism established under the Paris Agreement, and jointly with the Technical Assistance, herein the SoC program seeks to achieve a paradigm shift in the model for demand-led climate finance. The GCF will make equity investments into special purpose vehicles that manage projects at the sub-national level. These projects will be designed to address both mitigation and adaptation needs of developing countries while both impact and economic returns. GCF and other third-party investors will make equity commitments to the Fund in exchange for a share of the project’s revenue stream, which is expected to be generated by 2030.

The Fund believes this target size is optimal to allow the Fund to invest in projects that address a broad array of climate change mitigation and adaptation needs at scale. The Fund will also provide technical assistance to support countries in developing their national strategies for climate action.

The dedicated Technical Assistance part of the programme constitutes this project. It is designed to first, establish proof of concept in the form of investment projects structured in ways that build clear mitigation and adaptation co-benefits (e.g., hydropower dams with NBS). The second phase will be a pilot project led by Pegasus Capital in the programme) to promote replicability and scalability. Second, this preliminary work will also establish a framework for monitoring and evaluation of projects designed to trace results so that they can be used by investment actors to track impact and synergies between mitigation-adaptation co-benefits incorporating livelihood improvement and habitat protection considerations. The third phase will be the scaling up of the pilot project with the support of relevant stakeholders (project developers, subnational authorities and regional investors) can participate in the necessary technical assistance to ensure that projects are designed for replicability and scalability.

The premise of the project is to go beyond environmental and social safeguards by purposefully design infrastructure investments to have clear mitigation results while enhancing climate resilience and at a higher level as part of wider development and economic strategies. Other positive impacts are also key and include 'social' as well as environmental benefits such as gender equality.

The project therefore entails the designing, developing and implementing of a complete value chain around infrastructure investments in mid-size infrastructure projects, linked to BSI as well as co-benefits relating to climate adaptation: those that can also serve as resilience building blocks.

## Capacity Building
Capacity building to enable entities build bankable projects that are centred around mid-size infrastructure projects, linked to BSI as well as co-benefits relating to climate adaptation: those that can also serve as resilience building blocks.

### Tools for Decision Making
Tools for decision making will be based on climate change mitigation metrics as well as related Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) and Nature-based Solutions (Nature-based Solutions) metrics, which will be used by the project team in order to identify investment opportunities.

## Study: Feasibility Studies
Feasibility studies on investment opportunities that enhance countries' capabilities as well as co-benefits relating to climate change adaptation: those that can also serve as resilience building blocks.

### Building Capacity
Building capacity to enable entities build bankable projects that are centred around mid-size infrastructure projects, linked to BSI as well as co-benefits relating to climate adaptation: those that can also serve as resilience building blocks.

### Tools for Decision Making
Tools for decision making will be based on climate change mitigation metrics as well as related Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) and Nature-based Solutions (Nature-based Solutions) metrics, which will be used by the project team in order to identify investment opportunities.

## The TA Component of the overall programme is critical for the feasibility and capacity building to make at pre-feasibility stage. It will also ensure that projects are aligned with the ScFIP criteria and the GCF’s priorities. The Pegasus Capital will have overall responsibility for implementing, overseeing, and coordinating the ScFIP process in order to identify investment opportunities and develop bankable projects (Component 1). Pegasus Capital's positive support will be central to TA intervention decision making. This is to ensure that the Technical Assistance will not invest in projects that are of no interest to the ScFIP Fund.

The Linked Pegasus Capital project, operates through an investment vehicle—the Global Subnational Climate Finance Investment Facility (ScFIF)—which will provide a range of financial products, importantly: philanthropies, private banks, private equities, pension funds, insurance families, family offices, and other institutional investors. The facility is climate resilient, low carbon infrastructure as a new asset class in mid-size infrastructure for private capital.

The Fund, as part of the Linked Pegasus Capital led project, will complement this Technical Assistance project to complete the infrastructure value chain by investing, in up to 45 sub-national middle class (2–75

Climate change is expected to increase risks to infrastructure at the subnational level given extreme weather events are already affecting infrastructure and livelihoods. The World Economic Forum, for instance, has recorded that infrastructure in many cities around the world is already being affected by climate change. In addition, investments in low carbon infrastructure can also push up emissions of greenhouse gases. Cities, and other subnational actors, around the world are struggling to fund even basic infrastructure projects, let alone more complex interventions such as those required for climate mitigation or adaptation.

This project aims to work within each of these categories of infrastructure to bring in climate mitigation opportunities and co-benefit needs that the proposed infrastructure interventions are expected to address. The Funding Proposal of the Global Subnational Climate Fund (SnCF Global) – Equity elaborates on the climate change mitigation and adaptation co-benefit needs that the proposed infrastructure interventions are expected to address.

The following information of this Funding Proposal Technical Assistance Facility to the SnCF Global focuses specifically on the role and contribution of Nature-based Solutions as part of climate smart, low carbon infrastructure investments.

Investments in order to co-construct less carbon intensive and more energy efficient can be enhanced through NBS. They are making sure they become more climate resilient and thus have the potential for more sustainability in the long-term. There are already cases where Nature-based Solutions can make infrastructures more sustainable.

Figure 2 below illustrates the integration of Nature-based Solutions into the design of infrastructure in a coastal zone. The design takes on a systems perspective, integrating the broader land and seascapes to integrate nature-based solutions with other types of infrastructure. This is an example of how Nature-based Solutions to protect the shoreline from extreme weather events in the illustration: protect infrastructure inland, while also drawing in a return from the investment itself.

These interventions, often perceived as bringing only conservation benefits, are used to reduce climate risks such as from hurricane or flood impacts, thus protecting the climate mitigation actions from other sectors.

While SHCNs can provide adaptation co-benefits, here we focus on a coastal zone example above all else:

a. Low-cost approaches to risk reduction in the face of rising sea levels, flooding and changes in storm frequency and intensity.*


* IPCC (2013) Managing the Risks of Extreme Events and Disasters to Advance Climate Change Adaptation: A Special Report of Working Groups I and II of the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change. New York, IPCC.
Numerous case studies show that communities that are protected by mangroves suffer a lower death toll (e.g., Diaz et al., 2013). Mangroves also provide other ecosystem services such as food provision and habitat for tropical storms (e.g., Badde et al., 2005). Mangroves help to reduce coastal erosion by slowing water currents and helping to trap sediment. The rate of mangrove loss is generally lower than the rate of sea level rise, so the area of mangroves has kept pace with moderate rates of sea level rise over thousands of years (Berts, 2012).

Other ecosystem services provided by mangroves and wetlands in coastal areas include:

- Wetlands, including mangroves, help reduce impacts from tidal surges that have become more prevalent in many coastal zones in the face of climate change, thus protecting nearby physical infrastructure such as roads and buildings.
- Lakes and wetlands regulate flows and store water, and thus critical for reducing the impact of droughts and floods. In addition, they provide a buffer against storm surges and waves which can thus reduce the need for having higher water storage capacity in built reservoirs in the face of prolonged dry periods or increased rainfall intensity.
- Wetland protection is needed to avoid the risk of limited water supplies in the face of climate change in Kenya’s semi-arid regions. This is particularly important given that Kenya has been declared a Disaster Emergency: Ecosystem Based Adaptation in Kenya’s Arid and Semi-Arid Rangelands*.
- Due to their ability to store carbon, wetlands can also help mitigate climate change by reducing the intensity and duration of increased heat in the face of climate change, thus protecting infrastructure and people from extreme heat. This is particularly important given that Kenya has been declared a Disaster Emergency: Strengthening Climate Resilience of Subsistence Farmers and Agricultural Plantation Communities in Arid and Semi-Arid Lands*.
- Wetlands can also help reduce risks to human health by reducing the spread of diseases such as malaria, especially with a more rapid dwindling of water supplies than was being seen from poor land use practices in the face of climate change scenarios. Using such Nature-Based Solutions has the added benefit of carbon sequestration.

Some mitigation options NDC measures, including in the coastal zone, such as restoration of natural ecosystems and soil carbon sequestration provide co-benefits such as improved biodiversity, soil quality, and local food security.

The types of NDCs include Nature-based Solutions in some form or another. At least 85% of Paris Agreement signatories include Nature-based Solutions in some form to help achieve their climate change mitigation goals. These solutions are also increasingly recognized for their role in climate change mitigation and adaptation, as well as their broader societal value in general. The Paris Agreement explicitly recognizes the importance of the conservation and enhancement, as aggregate of sinks and sources, of all natural ecosystems, including terrestrial, inland water, coastal, and marine ecosystems.

Based on the review of the first round of NDCs, one study concluded that 77% of NDCs reference current or planned afforestation/reforestation activities. Of these, 50% are quantitative targets (e.g., planting 10 million trees), with 20% of which are quantifiable. 8% expressed in tons of carbon dioxide equivalent. 55% of NDCs include forest-related adaptation measures, and 39% include specific references to coastal ecosystems. These NDCs are conditional upon international support. More specifically for the coastal zone: 19% of Parties with coastal ecosystems include these habitats in the climate mitigation component of their NDCs. 59% in planning or zoning-related activities.

Box 1 provides examples of climate-proofed infrastructure that have the potential for wider replication and upscaling.


| Box 1 Examples of climate-proofed infrastructure |
| --- |
| Coastal hazards will be exacerbated by an increase in the average intensity, magnitude of storm surge and precipitation extremes due to the effects of climate change, as the IPCC BROC reports. Sustainable management of ecosystems can play an important role in integrated adaptation responses by both reducing coastal erosion and increasing sedimentation. |
| **Breakwater oyster reefs**, Kuthubdia Island, Bangladesh: Over four seasons, it was found that the 0.6metre high reefs attenuated wave energy by 95-100% when water levels were elevated up to 0.5m, whereas they only reduced wave energy by 28% at a water level of 3m above mean sea level (MSL) and no wave height. Sediment accumulated on the landward side of the reefs, with erosion rates 54% lower than without the reef. The reefs also provided habitat for fish species such as *Pteroscion* and *Parapercis*, which are important food sources in Bangladesh. The reefs also provide a nursery ground for coastal salt marshes to flourish, which further stabilised adjacent sediment. Sea level is projected to continue beyond 2100 at all scenarios for the IPCC BROC projections. These reefs are thus serving to protect against future sea-level rise and associated tidal waves and storm surges. The breakwater oyster reefs provided the additional benefit of species habitat, enabling more availability also of food. The reef has been estimated to provide a net annual economic benefit of approximately 50 gallons of water each day (Resilient Communities) – in addition to the protection from storms. |

The North-East Kalimantan coastal area is increasingly vulnerable to sea-level rise and the more severe weather events associated with climate change. The Green Climate Fund (GCF) is financing Roodt's Tarakan area’s Selva Shrimp Indonesia project, an IUCN supported initiative of its Blue Natural Capital Finance Facility, which is changing the way shrimp is produced, using Nature-based Solutions. Instead of clearing mangroves and other coastal vegetation to make room for shrimp farms, this project creates a more climate resilient shrimp farm alongside coral sequestration. Success at the 100ha test plot in 2015 led to the scaling up of the project to 370 ha. The total carbon stock in the project area is estimated as 48 t CO₂/ha, with an additional potential for climate change mitigation potential is estimated at 17 t CO₂/ha within 10 years.

A risk assessment & geological survey of Halis’s coastline identifies as greatest climate risks (1) rising sea levels, (2) increased frequency and intensity of storms & flooding and (3) severe drought. The adjacent mangrove forests spreading over 8,000 ha, protect the critical infrastructure that will remain in place for decades to come. The mangroves also provide habitat for the island’s largest mangrove forest, which in turn, protects its greenfield waste-to-energy infrastructure project from climate exacerbated weather events, a project led by Montrose based engineering firm Thales. This project is expected to sequester 1 million tons of CO₂ over its lifetime and will be a model for scaling out the use of mangroves to improve ecosystem services and carbon sequestration in areas where deforestation in these areas, while conserving the mangrove forest protects the nearby infrastructure from storm surges.

In Phewa Lake watershed, Nepal, there were 179 erosion events recorded along 123 km of rural roads, averaging a loss of 4.5 tons of sediment per kilometre of earthen roads or 58 t/130 ha soil released in the watershed, higher than the rates of erosion seen in the past. Climate change is considered to be increasing the frequency and intensity of heavy rainfall events which will further amplify the impacts of climate change over and above those caused from poor land-use practices alone. In Tilaurakot, Nepal, the Conservation Finance Initiative (CFI) project has reduced soil loss by 90% in the last five years. The CFI is a conservation investment initiative managed by IUCN and funded by the Global Environment Facility (GEF). It aims to provide financial incentives for land users to adopt sustainable land management practices that improve biodiversity, land degradation, climate mitigation and adaptation, ecosystem and community resilience with the ultimate goal of reducing soil erosion in Nepal. The CFI is also being used as a unique learning opportunity for conservation finance through a Coalition for Private Investment in Conservation (CPI) pilot project in India. The CFI has been successful in demonstrating that conservation finance can be an effective tool to reduce soil loss and improve land use.

While Box 1 illustrates a few examples of using Nature-based Solutions to climate proof infrastructure projects, there are many more examples from around the world. For instance, the Conservation Finance Initiative (CFI), a conservation investment incubator, managed by IUCN and funded by GEF, has been successful in demonstrating that conservation finance can be an effective tool to reduce soil loss and improve land use. The CFI is being used as a unique learning opportunity for conservation finance through a Coalition for Private Investment in Conservation (CPI) pilot project in India.

Other related initiatives include the Andrgreen Fund (andrgreen fund), led by IDH, the sustainable trade initiative, to invest in deforestation-free commodity production with a focus on tropical forests and peel at scale. The Andrgreen Fund has invested $10 M USD into 2014 and 2015. In addition, the GCF has closed six conservation investment deals totalling 200 M USD. The Conservation Finance Alliance has been established to support the scaling up of the CFI model in other countries. This is being done by reducing the recurrent costs of conservation, for instance in protected areas. The Athelia Climate Fund catalyzes investments in land use projects that deliver reduction in carbon emissions. Gold Standard has been selected as a partner to help scale up the CFI model and reduce the recurrent costs of conservation.

The amount of private sector investment in nature is gaining momentum. However, many of these initiatives focus on leveraging conservation finance with biodiversity conservation as the main focus. For this reason, it is important to consider how to best integrate nature-based solutions into climate proof low carbon infrastructure (such as Box 1 above), not at a scale that can bring a paradigm shift.

A recent study by World Bank and others shows that the cost of inaction, with respect to better land-use planning, climate-resilient coastal infrastructure and sustainable management of natural infrastructure, exceeds USD 2 trillion in the percent of the global GDP. This is equivalent to 10% of projected global GDP growth over this period. It is important, doubling the median benefit-cost ratio (World Bank 2019). Other analyses also show similar conclusions that the cost of inaction is high and that nature-based solutions can be a key component for climate financially attractive.

Zurich Flood Resilience Alliance is a multi-sectoral partnership focusing on finding practical ways to help communities in developed and developing countries strengthen their resilience to flood risk, especially given the additional risks of increased flooding in a “with-climate change” scenario. They recommend that the focus should be on integrating nature-based solutions into infrastructure projects at the subnational level and where possible with adaptation co-benefits.

While each sector of focus carries unique barriers (for the full list of barrier, please see the Fund Proposal for the Global Subnational Climate Fund (SNCf Global) - Equity Illustration or Gold Standard certified projects), which prior projects demonstrate can be overcome, and serve as models for wider scale-up and adoption. It is important to consider integrating nature-based solutions into infrastructure projects at the subnational level.

## B

### Higher design tolerances of grey infrastructure potentially leading to long term project failure or unforeseen upgrade costs (e.g. reduced flood protection benefits of levees with higher rates of sea level rise or reservoir storage capacity driven by upstream water or sediment migration).

- **Lack of granular, site-specific knowledge of specific infrastructure risks and nature-based risk mitigation options at the subnational level**.
- **Lack of confidence within the broader audience and the finance community in particular that Nature-Based Solutions will provide the predicted protection and ecosystem benefits.**
- **Lack of technical assistance to support the development of integrated Nature-based Solutions and infrastructure projects and offering blended financing solutions.**
- **Lack of business models and arrangements capable of channelling finance to stakeholders concerned; and**
- **Lack of partnership models for delivering these projects**.

These barriers to investment in low carbon, climate resilient infrastructure at the subnational level can vary across countries and regions depending on local context and governance structures. Addressing these barriers calls for a number of measures to be supported from technical assistance funding:

### First, establishing proof of concept in the form of investment projects designed for clear mitigation action, alongside climate resilience (blueprints) so that they can be used by investment actors to promote replicability and scalability.

Second, developing a suite of concept models, the development of distinct methods and granular tools to trace results so that they can be used by investment actors to track measurable climate mitigation impact as well as climate resilience/adaptation co-benefits... between digital solutions and physical infrastructure. This will also include the development of a business case.

Proof of concept from actual deals resulting from the technical assistance from this project are expected to showcase a better cost-benefit value for projects. This will change perceptions of investors and investors will be more willing to invest in these projects. The success of these projects will not only be part of an analysis of return rates but also depend on the enabling environment for investment, including the availability of technical assistance, access to finance, adequate institutional support as well public policies, regulatory frameworks and incentive mechanisms to promote integrated Nature-based Solutions and climate-resilient infrastructure investments are key and how they can be scaled up. This will also include the development of a business case and inclusive measures in the wider society to ensure that project deals are indeed in line with social needs. Ultimately, this will lead to more investment in low carbon, climate resilient infrastructure as well as to which climate resistant, low carbon infrastructure becomes mainstream among local authorities in the participating countries, globally.

level is key: 70% of known climate solutions are located within the remit of subnational authorities.* Significant additional investment is needed in this sector to achieve the climate goals of the Paris Agreement. The SnCF Global presents a positive disruption on how subnational climate projects should be shaped and financed, by unlocking significant new climate solutions and scaling them up at the highest level of rigour and quality.

The Fund is designed to overcome private investors’ project and investment barriers that lead to chronic underinvestment in climate mitigation and adaptation projects at the sub-national level. At the deal size of $5-$75 million project funding, Thousands of high merit sub-national projects are bypassed by commercial financial institutions due to their small scale and lack of creditworthiness. The SnCF Global is designed so that the GCF will unlock both public investors (MDBs, DFIs, Sovereign Funds… ) and even more important, private investors (pension funds, insurance funds, family offices, private banks, philanthropists, high net worth individuals…). This is a first-of-its-kind initiative to unlock private investment in sub-national projects that do not have access to attractive investment vehicles to invest at sub-national level. This is the first time an investment vehicle has been created for both public (40%) and private sector (60%) funding at scale to de-risk sub-national middle size infrastructure projects.

The Fund is designed to unlock significant new climate solutions and create scalable models for public and private investors to finance them. The SnCF Global is a unique opportunity in the area of increasing interest for capital investors for some time, but has yet to achieve its full potential. The readiness shown by the financiers of the SnCF Global (GCF, BNP Paribas, and other non-discounted investors) demonstrates the willingness and eagerness to ensure innovative finance solutions for NSIs don’t just remain limited to pilot projects. The SnCF Global is a unique opportunity in the area of increasing interest for capital investors.

The SnCF Global creates additioally both at fund level and at project level. At Fund level, the use of a blended equity/loan, fund-impact certification, exclusive subnational and mid-size project focus are all designed to direct private capital to projects that otherwise would not receive investment. At project level, the use of a blended equity/loan, fund-impact certification, exclusive subnational and mid-size project focus in project design and selection will ensure that otherwise technically sound projects become bankable.

The ultimate beneficiaries of the SnCF are the citizens living in the subnational jurisdictions where investments will occur. They will gain access to improved services with lower costs, which can have positive impacts on health outcomes and improve ecosystems, in terms of waste management, air and water quality, as well as access to clean energy and energy efficiency.

Immediate beneficiaries are the sub-national authorities controlling procurement and project development. These authorities will be able to access a new financing vehicle that is designed specifically to attract long-term and stable green investments. The positive Climate and SDG impacts will link to and directly

### B

#### Public-private consortium: The Fund will be managed by GCF-accredited Pegasus Capital Advisors. The Technical Assistance (TA) will be managed by GCF-accredited IUCN. The TA will provide financial and technical assistance to project developers, governments, and other stakeholders in preparing the Green Climate Fund Measure. Report and Verify green sub national projects' impacts; thus helping NDCs & national and sub- national governments to better understand the conditions to design, develop and implement projects that are aligned with their climate change priorities and commitments. The TA will ensure viable, adhere to strong environment and social safeguards, and integrate N3 where possible. R20 Regions of the World will be a key partner in this regard. Pegasus Capital Advisors will provide financial and technical certification. BNP Paribas will be the depositary / Custodian Bank as well the distributor of the Fund. The Fund will have its first closing with the GCF as the anchor investor as soon as possible, and the remainder (of the capital) will be raised from other investors over time, on a rolling basis approximately one year after the initial closing with GCF.

Together, the consortium members bring diverse and relevant expertise to achieve the Fund's objectives. With this expertise, they will help to demonstrate the development of projects, throughout the length of the project development and financing value chain; bringing together finance, engineering and construction companies, financial institutions, investors, governments, knowledge, coordinating efforts of major players, and building capacity, understanding and trust.

**Blended finance model: de-risk for private capital. Investment risk is greatly reduced with GCF becoming a public-private partner in the project development process. The Fund will be structured as a blended finance vehicle, notably those that "blend" capital in such a way to match the risk and return that can be expected at each stage in the project development and investment value chain with the expectations of different investors. This will allow for the creation of a more efficient and effective financing structure. The lower risk profile is needed to attract private capital. Doing so is essential to achieve the Paris Agreement's goal of limiting global warming to well below 2°C above pre-industrial levels by 2030. Additional worldwide annual investments of $4 trillion USD will be needed to achieve the SDGs by 2030. The annual investment gap in developing countries is expected to remain high at around 2.5 trillion USD and will need to be addressed if we are to meet the goals set out in the Paris Agreement, as well as the Objectives or the SDGs.

**Exclusive sub-national focus: There is an urgent need to act on climate and development finance at the sub-national level where there is pervasive underfunding of mitigation and adaptation projects in cities, counties, regions and states. 50%-80% of needed climate mitigation opportunities are however at sub-national levels. The Fund will be designed to support projects that have a clear pathway to financial, social and environmental viability alignment. This is especially important from the investor point of view, to manage project risk. The Fund will only invest in public – private sub-national climate projects for which there is a clear pathway to financial, social and environmental visibility, alignment

---

and reporting products in support of country-driven priorities and can be integrated into national reporting on Nationally Determined Contributions (NDCs) and SDG. From a TA perspective, awareness, training and capacity building of public authorities will ensure that enabling conditions for green infrastructure investments are in place. This includes the availability of technical expertise, access to knowledge and tools to fast-track procurement and project development. This should create a longer, and more stable pipeline of projects, which can be scaled up to meet national targets.

Mid-size project investment criteria: The Fund targets projects with a total cost of $5-275 million dollars. The investment window for many sub-national projects is too often. Local green infrastructure projects fall between the grant funding threshold of most public funds and the loan size limit of most development banks, which are too large for the $5 million threshold of most public funding. The transaction cost is considered too high compared to return on investment. The Fund fills this critical gap. The Fund is demand-driven, climate resilient, and can be scaled up to meet national targets.

The Fund will support projects that have a clear focus on adaptation and resilience, especially for "mid-size" projects, as well as making a tangible link to NDCs.

ESMS and global standards will backstop Fund design, project selection and impact. The Fund is designed with the following principles in mind: climate change mitigation, adaptation, and sustainability co-benefits. The Fund’s rigorous ESMS and certified design incorporate best-practice international environmental and social management systems (ESMS) for projects that address gender conditions, gender equity, and anti-corruption. etc. These same criteria will be applied to project selection and investment decisions in addition to local registration, key financial data at the level of key country stakeholders, and a clear link to NDCs and SDG 13, and high-impact projects with respect to mandatory safeguards and certified impact offsets (where applicable for a 3 per cent project). TA materials will be public goods to ensure uptake by other investment funds. Capacity transfer will be sustained through the initial support in establishing Technical Assistance Hub.

Activities:

Investment: The main investment activities include fund raising for private capital investors into the Fund, deal sourcing through the project pipeline (supported via the TA), and investment decisions and management once deals are selected. Specific tasks may include:
- Develop dedicated blended finance allowing both public finance institutions such as sovereign funds (SFs) and development banks, as well as private sector entities like family offices, pension funds and private banks to invest according to their risk and risk-taking profile.
- Identify and develop a project pipeline using an up-front approach directly sub national, which maximize positive climate and SDG impact. 
- Conduct due diligence on potential investment opportunities and green infrastructure projects meet the investors’ requirements so that they become bankable.
- Measure and report on environmental, social and economic impacts thanks to Gold Standard, the Fund’s third-party climate and SDG certification partner.

Capacity Development: The SCIF will leverage the extensive expertise and resources of the consortium members to develop tools, awareness, training and other means of capacity transfer. TA resources will

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.1 PAGE 18 OF 77

## Impact - Certification: The Gold Standard, an international market standard used by ~7,000 projects in 50 countries, will be applied to the Green Climate Fund and project design. The use of this standard, which is aligned with the Fund’s ESMs, will ensure project safeguards, mitigation, and adaptation impacts (SDG) are implemented. This means that if a project was certified as meeting these criteria when they were designed, then those safeguards were implemented, and that projected mitigation and adaptation/resilience impacts (SDGs 3, 5, 6, 7, 8, 11, 13, 14, 15) are achieved.

### Scale - Subnational/High-Level Transfer: The SnCF Program within the Technical Assistance will include 35 subnational regional hubs in line with the geographic scope of the Fund. Already, the SnCF Global is positioned to engage regionally, through an active network of Latin America capital cities and regions connected by a shared approach and common tools. This will be further strengthened for Africa, the Commonwealth, and in particular with southern national and regional certified GCF entities within which regional hubs will be established. These hubs will be part of a larger SnCF Global network that is working, within the framework of the African Union with ADB, Afreximbank, African Trade Development Bank, African Corporate Bank and other national foreign Funds to this end. The hubs will function as part of an integrated system for subnational high-level transfer, enabling each region independently to develop bankable, high-integrity projects and to attract global climate finance.

#### SnCF Initiative Target High-Level Outputs:
1. Investment: $750 million in blended public/private capital is invested in approx. 35 subnational projects in 20-25 countries.
2. Additional $4 billion is invested in Technical Assistance to support 35-60 high-integrity, bankable projects, integrating NSIs where feasible. Note that this aspect of the SnCF will be undertaken as part of the separate TA funding proposal.

#### SnCF Initiative Target High-Level Outcomes:
Financial: The SnCF blended finance model catalyzes private investment in new, subnational climate projects and provides a cost-effective and efficient pathway for private capital to support additional, high-impact projects linked to ecosystem conservation.
Technological: The SnCF shared approach and open-source resources and training build bankable, high-integrity project proposals that are ready to attract the needed financial capital.
Climatic and Ecological: CO2e and water loss, ecosystem and related NSIs are achieved at scale. Impacts are certified to provide assurance for donors and proof of concept for future investors.
Gender and Social: Every SnCF project must implement, at minimum, a gender sensitivity framework (SDG 5) and a social inclusion framework (SDG 10). In addition, projects will be required to include at least two additional resilience/ adaptation measures (SDGs 12,3,6,7,8,11,13), including those related to ecosystems and natural solutions (SDGs 14,15). Impacts will be verified by an independent 3rd party.

## Institutional and Regulatory: Subnational projects support country priorities and regional capacity is sustained through dedicated hubs. The TA and hubs provide an opportunity for S&CF capacity transfer on technical elements, standards, policies and other best practice, and to learn about the same from regional and subnational partners.

### Goal
Institutional and regulatory capacity of national authorities and subnational entities is strengthened in order to ensure that they can effectively implement climate change adaptation projects and programs. This includes strengthening institutional arrangements for project preparation and implementation, as well as ensuring that national authorities are able to engage with the international community on climate change adaptation.

### Beneficiaries
- National authorities
- Subnational entities
- Project proponents
- Financial institutions

### Outcomes
- Institutional and regulatory capacity of national authorities and subnational entities is strengthened in order to ensure that they can effectively implement climate change adaptation projects and programs.
- Strengthening institutional arrangements for project preparation and implementation, as well as ensuring that national authorities are able to engage with the international community on climate change adaptation.

### Outputs
- Institutional and regulatory capacity of national authorities and subnational entities is strengthened in order to ensure that they can effectively implement climate change adaptation projects and programs.
- Strengthening institutional arrangements for project preparation and implementation, as well as ensuring that national authorities are able to engage with the international community on climate change adaptation.

### Activities

| --- | --- | --- |
| Capacity building workshops | High | High |
| Technical assistance (TA) | Medium | Medium |
| Financial support for capacity building and technical assistance | Low | Low |

### Drivers
- Strengthening institutional arrangements for project preparation and implementation, as well as ensuring that national authorities are able to engage with the international community on climate change adaptation.
- Institutional and regulatory capacity of national authorities and subnational entities is strengthened in order to ensure that they can effectively implement climate change adaptation projects and programs.

### Goal Statement
To strengthen institutional and regulatory capacity of national authorities and subnational entities in order to ensure that they can effectively implement climate change adaptation projects and programs. This includes strengthening institutional arrangements for project preparation and implementation, as well as ensuring that national authorities are able to engage with the international community on climate change adaptation.

### Projects
- **Project 1:** $20 million allocated to support capacity building workshops.
- **Project 2:** $5 million allocated to technical assistance (TA).
- **Project 3:** $10 million allocated to financial support for capacity building and technical assistance.

### Key Drivers
- Strengthening institutional arrangements for project preparation and implementation, as well as ensuring that national authorities are able to engage with the international community on climate change adaptation.

## B

### Project/programme description (max. 2000 words, approximately 4 pages)

Define the project/programme. Describe the proposed set of components, outputs and activities that lead to the expected Fund-level impact and outcome results. Components should reflect the project/programme logic model.

This should be consistent with the financing by component in section C.2., the results and performance indicators provided in section E.5., and the implementation timetable in annex 5.

Referring to the feasibility study, describe why this set of interventions was selected instead of alternative solutions. Explain how the project/programme will ensure that the interventions are delivered supported in a sustainable manner. Also identify trade-offs of the selected interventions, if applicable.

For Enhanced Direct Access (EDA) proposals and projects/programmes with financial intermediation under EDA, please refer to section 10 for further guidance on programme description.

The SnCF programme comprises both the SnCF Fund project and the technical assistance project. Its main outcome is to enable the implementation of approximately 30-35 low carbon and resilient infrastructure projects in a cost-effective manner through the use of a single vehicle, the SnCF Global. The programme's overall activities are focused on delivering the project pipeline (including via the Technical Assistance), de-risking investments through the structure of the Fund, measuring impacts (via the Technical Assistance), and ensuring that the projects deliver climate resilience and adaptation at scale. This Technical Assistance project delivers the pipeline, which will include bankable projects that have climate resilience integrated into the project designs of measurable climate mitigation impact.

### Components of the Proposal Studies

- **Component TA1: Climate Mitigation Infrastructure Projects**  
  Development of feasibility studies on infrastructure investment opportunities that enhance countries' climate mitigation action, with adaptation co-benefits. These studies shall serve as the basis for SnCF Global's investment decisions.

- **Outcome TA1**: Climate mitigation infrastructure projects are additional, commercially viable, screened, and selected for a high level of SDG impacts, ES safeguards, and, where possible, include a Nature-based Solutions (NbS) component.

- **Component TA2: Capacity Building**  
  Capacity building to enable countries build bankable projects that are certified as climate change mitigation action with adaptation co-benefits. On a programmatic level, various stakeholders in need of knowledge transfer.

- **Outcome TA2**: Relevant stakeholders have the information and capacity needed to support the development of bankable projects at scale and those that enable fast-track growth of mitigation projects on a subnational level, as well as to foster up-scaling and replication of investment deals. Work to develop a pipeline of projects for component 1.

- **Component TA3: Tools and Metrics**  
  Development of Climate Mitigation, Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs), and Nature-based Solutions (NbS) indicators and tools that promote climate resilience and mitigation. Those shall be applied to identify and support the design and development of bankable projects for component 1 and eventually by the SnCF Fund project to monitor investment results.

For further details regarding this process, actors and responsibilities between Fund and Technical Assistance at Annex B6 of the Funding Proposal for the SnCF Global - Equity. The Technical Assistance will distribute at the approx. same ratio as the Fund.

In terms of project feasibility and development work, the Technical Assistance foresees the assess and support up to 50 projects. This project aims to have 70% of the projects (approximately 35) to make it from pre-feasibility to financial structuring phase - led by Pegasus Capital for the SnCF Docks). The feasibility work falls primarily into the two first phases of the project development stages, namely early-stage sourcing and project development.

Any structuring done for the Fund is covered by Pegasus. general structuration done for the project development stage is covered by the TA. Financial structuring is covered by financial elements such as integrating NBS into the deal, by the TA while financial structuring is part of the Fund.

IUCN will support the early sourcing efforts through technical advice and screening of the project pipeline including initial assessment of potential projects.

A Project Accelerator Committee will decide on how the funds from the Technical Assistance will be spent. It is composed of R2D and IUCN, as voting members. A representative from the SCfF (legal entity to be established) will also participate in this committee. The decisions made by the PAAC will be captured in the proceedings for the PAF Committee meetings. Technical ad-hoc experts (guarantors of the technical know-how) will advise the decision-making process but not have any decision powers. They will join the PAAC to provide advice on specific aspects such as project design, selection criteria, etc.

The PAFs do not have decision power on what funds from the Technical Assistance will be spent on the project, but are pre-selecting (as per Activity TA1:1.3) Select projects for technical assistance support, and get final approval of funding from the PAAC. The PAAC will provide final approval regarding such as example related to Activity TA 2.1: Perform on site evaluation, stakeholder meetings and pre-feasibility studies of selected projects.

The Project Accelerator Committee will review and provide final approval regarding fund from the Technical Assistance on projects that have been originated and technically reviewed by the following (yet not yet PAF) Project Accelerator Facilitators (PAFs):

* **IUCN managed Blue Natural Capital Financing Facility (BNCFF) as well as the newly emerging Conservation Finance Initiative (CFI)**

R20's initially established Project Accelerator Facilities (PAFs) will be expanded on to ensure that identified projects meet bankability criteria and, thanks to Gold Standard's criteria, meet all safeguarding and ESMS compliance requirements. R20 is further partnering with Swiss firms Alpis (international energy utility) and BIG Group (international energy engineering firm), to create the Energy Project Facility (EPF) a mechanism for financing projects in the renewable sector. The Technical Assistance envisages to have 1 PAF or c. one for each of the seven selected investment sectors.

PAFs will prioritise projects and deliver feasibility studies, presented based on the Green Climate Fund. The PAFs are responsible for project preparation and provide technical guidance. They authorise the use of technical assistance funds for project development. It is important to note that EGIS, Alpis, and BG have jointly with R20 financed the existing PAFs in the total amount of 2 MUSD to successfully develop projects in this area.

The technical assessment process of the PAFs has been designed to mitigate the risks of non-completion at every stage of the feasibility study. A funnel process with defined TA criteria for the selection of projects for funding is used. The PAFs will be responsible for project preparation and provide technical guidance on the other hand by policy-makers, engineers and investors involved by the portfolio – and regular screening from the PAF Committee ensure a best use of resources and selection of projects. This process will also ensure that all projects are in line with the Green Climate Fund's Environmental and Social Management System (ESMS) to ensure proper implementation of the ESMS to the entire 'value chain' from the project concept through investment and asset management. ## B

**public and private investors who invest in the SnCF Global for maximizing impact (environment/financial) in addition to seeking financial returns and provide investors with ESG ratings of the investment process.**

The project team screens the project proposal, signs an exclusively agreement for the feasibility study and assessment of the project, and submits a request for feasibility to the respective PAF Committee who evaluates the project. If accepted, the PAF Committee sets up a field mission for technical experts to assess the situation on the ground and document the findings in a feasibility report. The PAF Committee then reviews the feasibility report and sends it back to the project team. The PAF Committee re-evaluates the project and approves (or declines) the undertaking of the feasibility study and assessment of the project. If approved, the project team proceeds with the preparation of the feasibility study, which includes elements such as the technical-economic analysis, the pre-PPA, pre-concessions EIA, identification of Nature-based Solutions components, land rights and detailed LofI from all stakeholders.

Strong focus is given to stakeholder's meetings, including civil society ensuring local acceptance. The PAF uses Gold Standard's consultation and engagement requirement guidelines to perform such meetings. The feasibility study also includes a gender analysis, which is an integral part of the project design that includes gender sensitivity, water assessment, and SDG impact pathways, mandatory stakeholder consultation, assessment of community Health, Safety and Working Conditions, anti-corruption.

The two remaining stages of the project development cycle are:

- **c. Implementation**

This project is not engaged in the investment stages of the SnCF programme. However, technical assistance will still support, where needed, SnCF and the developer to ensure a smooth transition into SnCF Due Diligence.

This project could, when applicable, provide support for the off-takers contracts, EPC and CM agreements, ensuring that the respective procedures have been performed.

## d. Reporting

The project is not engaged in the reporting stage of the SnCF programme. This project will however, in its early stages, contribute to development of SDG and Nature-based Solutions impact metrics, tools and indicators, to be used and applied in the reporting stage of the Fund (see Component 3 and related activities). Given also the shorter duration of the Technical Assistance with respect to the actual Fund, the technical assistance will not be involved in the preparation of the project's annual progress report.

Given the set-up of the whole programme, the actual SDG verification of the projects and the Fund, will be outside of the purview of the Technical Assistance. The verification will be paid for by the projects budgets or by the Fund, and completed by a certified, third-party verification organisation.

#### Output TAZ:1. Training of project developers to increase understanding of safeguard requirements, ESMS requirements, SDG and Nature-based Solutions criteria, and other investment requirements for high-integrity, bankable project design and delivery.

Activities TAZ-1.3: Activities will raise awareness, help identify potential projects and provide for enabling conditions for green infrastructure investments

#### Output TAZ:2. Best practices, guidelines and tools are developed, disseminated and applied

Activity TAZ 2.1: Production of best practices, guidelines and tools for replication and upscaling.

A core component of the SnCF programme, implemented via TA, is capacity building. It will focus on three main areas:

- Clustered technical workshops with project developers and intermediaries
- Subnational workshop with local authorities
- Regional workshops with regional financiers

Capacity-building is a key element in achieving the 2030 Agenda for Sustainable Development. Sustainable Development Goal Target 17 of the 2030 Agenda for Sustainable Development is the dedicated target to capacity building, and it is also mentioned under other targets. The 2030 Agenda for Sustainable Development and capacity-building in developing countries to support national plans to implement all the sustainable development goals (SDGs) are also mentioned under target 17.8 in the context of ensuring full operationalization of the “technology bank and science, technology and innovation capacity building” component of the Addis Ababa Action Agenda.

Furthermore, the 2030 Agenda deals with the means required for implementation of the Goals and targets. Activities TAZ-2.1 will contribute to this by providing technical assistance in developing and implementing the transfer of environmentally sound technologies to developing countries on favourable terms including on concessional and preferential terms, as mutually agreed.

Projects envisaged as part of the SnCF programme will not be trial, and will need dedicated financial resources for their implementation. The Technical Assistance (TA) component will provide viability tools on project developers, that the technical assistance plans to accelerate project design and bring up-front projects to a stage where they can be submitted to project development (PD) to ensure ES safeguard requirements and the potential to engage with EMS, SDG and Nature-based Solutions are understood. These trainings will also focus on key investment parameters, including financial viability, risk assessment, and environmental and social safeguards. The participants will be selected based on geographical and thematic aggregation of project developers. These trainings will be conducted in a face-to-face format (short to medium-term training workshops, courses), E-Learning courses, webinars, e-learning modules, online discussion forums, video conferencing, webinars, lectures, etc., real world experience (field visits) as well as experience sharing (discussion groups, expert’s perspectives).

The Technical Assistance, with the support from UNITAR, will administer a questionnaire at the conclusion of each activity to obtain participant reactions on a number of key measures, including job relevance, necessary skills and knowledge, and overall satisfaction. The results of this survey will be shared if have been met. Results from the monitoring and evaluation of the project will be communicated to all parties involved through a form of a project completion report which will record findings, conclusions, and recommendations.

A second set of training workshops will be conducted with subnational actors, primarily with local authorities. Subnational workshops will be held to ensure an increased understanding of the SnCF

- The International Union for Conservation of Nature (IUCN) as the Accredited Entity (AE), IUCN is a membership Union uniquely composed of both government and civil society organisations. It provides public goods, including policy advice, technical expertise, capacity building, monitoring progress, economic development and nature conservation to take place together. Created in 1948, IUCN has evolved into the world’s largest and most diverse environmental network. With a global reach, IUCN works in more than 130 countries and territories and the input of over 10,000 experts. IUCN is the global authority on the status of the natural world and the measures needed to safeguard it.

The project’s Executing Entities (EEs) are R20, IUCN and Gold Standard. Pegasus Capital will not receive funding from this project as it is a financial intermediary that has been engaged by IUCN under Subsidary Agreements in the form of IUCN’s standard implementing agreements.

- R20 Regions of Climate Action (R20) is a not-for-profit international organisation established in 1997. Since its inception, R20 has grown to become an international network of 35 regions led by President Obama and many Heads of regions from around the world, to replicate world-wide what it was able to implement in California: pro climate policies, regulations, programmes and investment.

Inspired the implementation of the Cancun Agreements in Mexico in 2011 as a response of a number of leading Regions, the United Nations Development Banks, Clean Development Mechanism, Adaptation and a number of NGOs to support subnational authorities around the world to develop green infrastructure projects. R20 will coordinate the execution of the project and lead Component 1.

# Green Climate Fund Funding Proposal 2 | Page 37 of 71

## Gold Standard (GS) - a not-for-profit that, since 2003, GS has been the assessment benchmark for over 1,700 high-impact climate and development programmes in 80 countries. Their flagship standard, Gold Standard for the Global Goals, sets a clear path from initial project design through final reporting. Best practice is embedded into the methodology to ensure that projects are independently verified or certified, per client requirements. Gold Standard methodology can be used to assess fund/project climate mitigation potential and contribution to climate finance, NDCs, and the UN Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs). The IUCN will play a role in ensuring that projects meet these standards, such as IFC safeguards or Article 9 of the Paris Agreement. Gold Standard will lead Component 3 of the project.

## IUCN will also play a role in leading the execution of Component 2 of the project.

### Implementation arrangement

#### Overview
IUCN is the GCF AE and will take on the oversight role of the funds received for the TA. IUCN will further administer the GCF technical assistance funds and provide for the appropriate implementing arrangements with the EEs. The designated IUCN Task Manager (TM) will review project progress as reported by the Project Manager (PM). The TM will be responsible for overseeing the PM in the execution of the project. The TM will carry out a desk analysis that involves reviewing project progress against work plans, procurement and delivery of goods and services, financial management, and other relevant issues. The TM will also serve as supervisory mission involving a team with requisite skills that further analyse technical and financial issues. The TM will also serve as a support and resource function to the PM and the EE to discuss ways to strengthen project implementation and ensure its directiveness, facilitation of achieving outcomes and impact, and efficiency. Finally, the TM will clear all reports.

#### Project Management Unit
The project will establish a Project Management Unit (PMU), coordinated and managed by R2O.
The PMU will be responsible for the coordination of all project activities funded by the project and undertaking relevant functions on the ground. The PMU is composed of technical experts and will be constituted to perform the following functions:
1. Consolidate the annual work plans submitted by the executing entity.
2. Responsible for monitoring funding flows as well as managing procurement.
3. Ensure coordinated delivery of the agreed project components and respective outputs and activities through the coordination of all partners, stakeholders and suppliers involved in project delivery.
4. Oversee the execution and timely dissemination of knowledge products with the support of relevant experts involved in the project delivery.
5. Ensure that all safeguards frameworks is used throughout the project and raise any potential safeguards violations.
6. Ensure operational staff and supervise their work programs.

The PMU will be composed as follows:
- Project Manager - Full-time (1 position)
- Admin Support - Full-time (1 position)
- Project Accountant (1 Position)

## Financial Flow

IUCN, as the project's Accredited Entity (AE), will request funds to be transferred from the GCF based on the project disbursement plan. All Executing Entities (EE) will propose project disbursement requests to the Project Management Unit (PMU), which is not an independent entity but rather a unit to manage the project within R20 at its headquarters in Switzerland. Other than the IUCN team engaged in execution of the project, all other Executing Entities (EE) will have a Subsidiary Agreement with IUCN as the AE. It should be noted that neither the SnCF Fund Manager nor General Partner will receive funds from this project for execution of activities.

Each Executing Entity will enter into service agreements with service providers to be procured during the project implementation phases


---
**Page 40**
---

There will be three models of funds flow: fund flow through

a) Subsidiary Agreements to R20 (in its project coordination role and lead for component 3) and to Gold Standard (as lead for component 3) by IUCN in the role as an AE. B.5 Justification for GCF fund request (max 1000 words, approximately 2 pages)

* Explain why the project/programme requires GCF funding. i.e. Why is the project/programme not currently being funded by other sources? Are there any other domestic or international sources of financing?
* Explain how the proposed financial instruments were selected in light of the proposed activities and the overall financing strategy. How does this compare to the financing strategy for similar projects in the past? How are they different?
* How are the financing amounts and prices determined? How does the concessionality of the GCF financing compare to other sources of funding? Provide a short market read on the prevailing of the pricing and/or financial markets for similar project/programmes.
* Justify why the level of concessionality (of the GCF financial instrument(s)) is the minimum required to make the investment viable. Consider the cost-benefit analysis and the proposed pricing with or without the concept of minimum concessionality? Who benefits from concessionality?

In your answer, please consider the risk sharing structure between the public and private sectors, the barriers to investment in emerging economies, and other reference relevant annexes, such as the feasibility study, economic analysis or financial analysis when appropriate.

This project needs GCF support for three primary reasons:

* The geographies and the Subnational scope where these projects will be built represent emerging economies where many institutional capital providers will not operate (or will not operate without partner institutions) due to the lack of infrastructure, capacity, and/or political stability (e.g. corruption).
* From a climate risk perspective, the world cannot wait for low-carbon development projects to scale up. The GCF can help to finance projects that are currently being held back by financial constraints but will be able to attract private sector investment once they have demonstrated their viability. The retrofit of streetlights with energy-saving LEDs will happen worldwide in the decades to come, but by accelerating this process and making it more cost-effective through GCF support, the number of cities supported by GCF, that transition (and the resulting pollution and GHG reductions) will take place at least a decade earlier.
* The GCF funding will help to support sustainable project design, including safeguarding, climate and sustainability impact MRV leading to new benchmark for such infrastructure.

Moreover, these projects are not currently being financed by the public and/or private sector because of:

* Uncertainties about the returns of investments, inadequate access to finance investments in. For example, irrigation equipment, water-efficient technologies, stress-resistant plants or infrastructural improvements that can be implemented at a relatively low cost but have high potential for impact. These projects may not have sufficient financial capacity, or have unattractive risk/revenue profiles. Benefits associated with such climate-resilient infrastructure are often long-term and/or indirect (e.g., reduced water scarcity, improved food security, reduced climate impacts). They may also be complex to evaluate when part of broader investments.
* Financial constraints: The public sector is often constrained by limited access to finance, lack of information or higher perceived risks because of the lack of track record, borrowers' inadequate collateral, or lack of capacity in appraising and financing non-traditional projects (and NBS).
* In the case of waste management, there are significant financial barriers. The public sector is often a "waste" feedstock and therefore allocated no funding for collection, sorting, recycling, and conversion; angering communities and leading to illegal dumping. This leads to increased costs in terms of health care and landfills operators, thereby incentivizing “out of sight, out of mind” buying or dumping


---
**Page 42**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.1 | PAGE 39 OF 77

## In the case of renewable energy generation, market and technological barriers have delayed project development, including cost (which only in recent years has become lower than fossil-fuel energy production) and base-load needs to be solar and wind are intermittent and cannot displace baseload fossil-fired power plants. The same is true for large-scale hydroelectric projects that rely on hydropower grids across countries.

## In the case of energy efficiency retrofit, each building being upgraded requires an investment grade energy audit to determine the most cost-effective measures. These audits can be conducted by firms as numerous and experienced in developing countries. In addition, even when such retorts pay for themselves over time, they are often not financially attractive enough to attract private sector capital that want to retrofit streetslights with more efficient LED technology, but don't have big capital to do so all at once or on a large scale.

## In all of these cases, the lack of a significant price on carbon slows stakeholder prioritization of these projects and makes them less attractive for investment.

### Knowledge gaps: Understanding climate vulnerability and risks and opportunities is essential to integrating climate change risks (or opportunities) into investment or financing decision-making. 

### Barriers to access to finance: The high cost of capital, especially in developing countries (high risk periods) can also affect business appetite to invest in climate resilience and low carbon investments, as well as the ability of governments to borrow at rates that would help mitigate climate-related impacts.

### High discount rates dominate investment decision-making

Development finance institutions and conventional investors may be willing to finance projects in these categories, but there has been a lack of project developers to create the special purpose finance vehicles (SPFs) or other innovative financing mechanisms. The Brazilian government, through its Green Climate Fund Advisors worked together on creating such an SPF in Brazil for cities to retrofit streetlights with LEDs and repay the loans from savings on energy and maintenance costs, guaranteed by each municipality's utility tax revenue. In Mali, the World Bank has been working with the government of Mali and the World Bank that initiated a similar financing mechanism; meaning investors had no opportunity to deploy capital in a similar way.

These dynamics also highlight the need for a mixture of grant funding via the TA and investment capital. In the Brazilian streetlight retrofit example, grant funding was needed by R20 to work with stakeholders in providing technical assistance (TA) on how to design the project, how to engage with local government managers in selection of technology providers; and to design the SPF before it could be offered to investors. In the case of utility-scale solar projects in Mali, grant funding was needed to support the work to bring together all parties involved in the project development process.

Moreover, in each case, even well-understood policies and technologies require a feasibility study to explore the potential for scaling up or replicating them at larger scales.

One thing that becomes clear in these types of projects is the importance of concessional finance in the capital structure. Energy efficiency retrofits, for example, typically generate financial returns below 10%, which makes it difficult to attract private sector investment without a significant level of grant or debt enhancement partner. This dynamic also restricts the level of ambition of such projects. For example, retrofit of some energy uses in buildings might yield results in 3 to 5 years and be very attractive to investors if they could get a concessional loan at 2% interest rate for 10 years.

The addition of concessional capital ensures that more energy efficiency retrofits will be adopted and thus the overall impact on carbon emissions will be greater.

Finally, when estimating the level of concessional finance needed in these types of projects it becomes clear that at least 20% of total committed capital is the most effective minimum contribution, because that level:

- Provides first loss risk (junior to conventional investors) that is the minimum requirement to attract conventional investors.
- Provides a practical blended cost of capital to make these types of projects viable.


---
**Page 43**
---

The primary beneficiary of the concessional capital is the municipality and its citizens, because these projects could not be undertaken without it, at minimum, would not be undertaken for many years.

Investors also benefit, because every capital source today is looking for “green,” “sustainable,” “impact,” and “socially beneficial” investments. The GCF has a strong track record of providing such investment criteria, which are reasonable considering the capital often comes from pensions and other socially beneficial sources.


More specifically, the Green Climate Fund is committed to additional climate and financial impacts within its own operations and in the projects in which it invests. SnG projects will only be forwarded to the investment committee for their consideration after first being assessed for additionality of their climate impacts. The GCF has a comprehensive methodology for assessing additionality that includes a summary table for a comprehensive summary of the climate and financial addtionality elements of the Fund.


In summary, the Fund is designed to deliver USD $750 million to new climate mitigation and adaptation projects that to date have not been funded. Financial addtionality is driven by the blended finance model in which the GCF provides concessional capital at 1% interest rate and a 20-year maturity. The Fund’s focus at the subnational level is that it often bypassed by conventional climate finance; and the Fund’s goal is to provide additional climate mitigation and adaptation projects at scale. For each project, Gold Standard provides financial addtionality tools developed by CDM and widely used across the carbon markets to assess if each project would be financially viable without investment revenue.


The Fund is likewise designed to create additional climate mitigation and adaptation impacts that go beyond what would otherwise have been achieved. This is done through a combination of ambitious reference benchmark standards for equipment, waste, energy, etc., ex-ante quantification of project impacts for climate (SDG 13) and a minimum two SDGs in which addtionality must be quantified. The Fund will also provide additional financial addtionality to projects that are focused on the Sustainable Solution (S3) with their vast potential to deliver additional climate mitigation support adaptation / resilience, and through the tools and methodologies offered through Technical Assistance to projects that would not otherwise be available.

Moreover, the strong stakeholder’s engagement ensures that the projects will be maintained in the long run with minimal government involvement.

**Page 44**

Moreover, all projects will be undertaken with the build-own-operate-transfer (BOOT) model, conveying ownership and operation of the asset to local municipalities or other stakeholders:

* For renewable energy generation projects, the power purchase agreement (PPA) will include post-completion performance guarantees and insurance wraps around technology providers to ensure the facility continues to operate as designed (from technical, financial and social perspectives).
* For waste optimization projects, land and feedstock agreements with municipalities will include performance guarantees and insurance wraps for technology providers to ensure project design goals.
* For energy efficiency retrofits, asset owners will be trained and provide assurances that projects are maintained to design specifications that include technical, financial and social goals.
* For all projects, a competitive bidding process will be awarded to credit-worthy contractors providing industry-leading performance guarantees and insurance wraps (around technology providers).
* Finally, adherence to the Gold Standard will ensure that project standards and goals are met through transparent measurement, reporting and verification (MRV) processes.

The ScNCF program will also collaborate closely with the newly established Climate Finance Initiatives (CFI), a concessionary investment mechanism managed by IUCN. As a GEF-based non-grant mechanism, the CFI will blend public and private finance and invest in deals that address climate change mitigation and adaptation, biodiversity loss and degradation, climate migration and adaptation, ecosystem and community resilience, and sustainable land management. The CFI is designed to be large enough for more risk-averse private capital and larger ticket size investments than the ScNCF Fund. It will also provide a platform for other Funds to invest in, and thus also accessible to the ScNCF Fund. This will be particularly relevant for the climate sector 2 of the ScNCF Global, namely restorative agriculture/aquaculture.
