D.1. PROJECT PROGRAMME DETAILS (approximately 1 page)

Specify the mitigation and/or adaptation impact, taking into account the relevant and applicable sub-criteria and assessment factors in the Fund’s investment framework.

For the purposes of this estimation, we assumed that 35 projects will be invested in by the SnCF Global, and delivering the expected climate and SDG impacts with the following theoretical breakdown. The project programme is structured as follows:

- 7 water and sanitation projects (each with a SnCF ticket of USD 20 million)
- 7 restoration agriculture projects (each with a SnCF ticket of USD 20 million)
- 7 solid waste optimisation projects (each with a SnCF ticket of USD 20 million)
- 7 forest conservation/renewed forests projects (each with a SnCF ticket of USD 10 million)
- 7 energy efficiency refurbishment projects (each with a SnCF ticket of USD 20 million)

Additional assumption can be found on Annex Z2.

SnCF expects to deliver multiple impacts, thereby contributing to several SDGs through its fully invested portfolio. The programme will contribute towards the following SDGs: SDG 6 (clean water and sanitation), SDG 7 (access to clean energy), SDG 8 (job creation), SDG 11 (sustainable communities) and SDG 13 (climate action). The programme also aims at contributing to SDG 9 (industry, innovation and infrastructure) in order to ensure resilience of the infrastructure deployed. On that end, SnCF is working actively with Rockefeller Foundation to develop a methodology for measuring the impact on SDG 9. SnCF is currently developing this methodology and is seeking to procure NBS when applicable. Uniquely, the SnCF will measure, report and certify impact for both climate mitigation, adaptation and sustainable co-benefits (SDGs) using Gold Standard methodology. Repeatable outcomes will be aligned with and support national level NDCs and SDGs.

Refer to figure below for a summary of total impacts.

The preliminary findings estimated that the Fund will reduce 4 million tons of CO2e per annum over the minimum 20-year life of the built infrastructure leading to a conservative reduction estimate of 11 million tonnes of CO2e over the lifetime of all projects. The programme is expected to create 35,000 jobs (reassessed every 5 years). Furthermore, the SnCF will create up to 20,000 jobs (with a set minimum of 4 female employees for a target of 10,000) and provide 2.5 million people with access to clean energy.

The programme is expected to deliver an average cost per tonne reduction of USD 38 ($/tCO2e) in energy plus solar PV projects), as well as 406 kWh saved per year (from LED street lighting projects). The programme will also deliver a direct impact over 17 million people living in urban areas to have improved access to basic services (waste collection, access to energy, better urban services and street lighting).

The impact potential of this programme via the Technical Assistance is strengthened by using capacities of IUCN as well as the ESF. The ESF will provide technical assistance in implementing mitigation and adaptation co-benefits projects. It is these agencies working together that will bring in the support to develop bankable deals that bring mitigation benefits and adaptation co-benefits. Combined with the experience of Pegasus Capital as a manager of an investment


---

fund, this joint partnership harnesses what can be truly transformational by taking advantage of very different AE capacities combined with EE capacities. In addition, the EE Gold Standard will be helping to integrate climate mitigation and adaptation benefits in the certification of mitigation and adaptation results from projects and programmes that are implemented under the Fund. This will help to ensure greater effectiveness. Climate change is indeed the core element in the project and within the programme. Projects and programmes supported by the Fund will have a strong track record of experience in mitigation and adaptation to climate change investment to join its team to ensure effective implementation.

#### Potential for scaling-up (e.g. multiples of initial impact size)

Successful implementation in Beneficiary Countries will make it easier to replicate in the Beneficiary countries, and this will be further supported by the fact that the Fund is expected to scale up to a number of regional DFIs and there is high interest for the concept with regional specific Sub-Funds. As mentioned above, the project will also contribute to the development of a global MRV system for the appropriation and mastering and the value chain and the blended finance vehicle as to be able to replicate and scale up.

Once the first operational model of the SiCfT has been well replicated in other geographies. This means that lessons learned and any success factors will be shared with relevant governments, donors and other stakeholders. The project will also contribute to the development of a global MRV system for the appropriation and mastering and the value chain and the blended finance vehicle as to widely proliferate. In terms of the SiCfT's impact, a first-in-class Measuring, Reporting and Verification (MRV) system will be developed that will allow for reporting and verification of SDG impacts at global level being set up by the UNFCCC and Gold Standard to publicly track SDG impacts.

The added market studies in the project enables the countries and sectors' market situation to be reviewed in order to identify the most appropriate instruments and mechanisms. This will help to ensure that best to improve the enabling environment for investments that would benefit national and local authorities priorities to take advantage of private sector interest and investment capabilities.

#### Potential for knowledge and learning

There is a high potential for knowledge and learning with this programme:

- First of all, R20, Pegasus and IUCN plan to set up a first-of-its-kind global measurement, reporting and verification (MRV) system that will be the backbone of the umbrella of the SiCfT in partnership with Gold Standard. This MRV system will be linked to a new global platform being set up by the UNFCCC and Gold Standard to publicly track SDG impacts at global level.
- Therefore, projects that get implemented under this programme may be publicly reporting their SDG impact through the SiCfT and the MRV system in partnership with Gold Standard. This will allow for comparability, and compliance, moreover as the project capacity will intensively include local consultants the technical, legal and financial know-how will remain in beneficiary countries allowing further replication.
- Contribution to the creation of an enabling environment

The R20 initiative is in itself an enabling environment which seeks to eliminate barriers to low-carbon and climate-resilient project solutions. By working directly with local authorities R20 assists with project identification and formulation. Afterwards, R20 works with the most promising of those identified projects through a process that includes feasibility studies, project development, and implementation. In collaboration with technology partners to fund the feasibility studies needed to make the projects investment ready. Even if projects are not immediately investable, they can be used as building blocks for larger investments. This is knowledge that can impact well beyond the primary targets of our programme.

The R20 value chain includes "training and capacity building" across all of the different value chain stages.

* Overall contribution to achieving resilient development pathways consistent with relevant national greenhouse gas reduction strategies and plans.
* Private investments in NGS have so far been limited. With the S4NCP programme, and IUCN as a partner, the R20 initiative is working on developing a portfolio of projects that can meet the goals of NGS, as well as mitigation. NGS is already part of many countries first round of NDCs and there is a growing interest to ensure NGS are part of the climate solutions (e.g. UN Climate Summit special track on NGS).

Preparation of detailed feasibility studies for climate change adaptation/migration projects is the direct scope of activities of many international financial institutions. This programme is however not creating the same type of project preparation facility. Rather, what makes this project unique is its focus on infrastructure development and investment in Subnational Climate Infrastructure (SCI). The aim is to protect infrastructure from climate change impacts and that bring mitigation benefits. While other preparation facilities exist for NGS, this is a new approach to financing adaptation and migration. It also unique proposition in the climate mitigation and adaptation arena. It is also unique in how it brings into this space actors such as BNP Paribas, Rockefeller Foundation, Edmond de Rothschild SA, whose engagement will be critical to the success of this programme. The R20 initiative has been working with these players to promote. These players while using this Fund as a first visit will use its model for future large scale regional and national climate adaptation programmes. For example, BNP Paribas is in discussions with Rockefeller Foundation as they seek to develop a multi billion umbrella blended finance climate initiative for COP 26. This approach has already gained some recognition, being selected as best climate finance city project of 2019 by the Global Environment Facility (GEF) and winning several financial best-practice platforms.

Intergovernmental bodies such as the Commonwealth and the Union for Mediterranean and some national governments have expressed interest in joining the R20 initiative. They are showing their interest in joining the consensus to learn and be able to replicate this unique strategy for respectively S4NCP and NGS. The R20 initiative is also exploring the potential of this approach as a new and important opportunity for Subnational Climate Infrastructure on the one hand and blended finance mechanism such unlocking private investors which are looking for secured climate finance vehicles in general and specifically for adaptation and migration projects. D.3 Sustainable development (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

Describe the wider benefits and impacts of the project/programme in relation to the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) as follows:

- **Environmental co-benefits**
- **Social and human health impacts**
- **Economic co-benefits**
- **Gender equality and empowerment impact**

As described in section 1.2, we expect the ScIFC (based on 35 projects) to contribute to several SDGs, notably SGD 3 (improved health), SGD 5 (gender mainstreaming), SGD 6 (clean water and sanitation), SGD 7 (access to clean energy), SGD 8 (job creation), SGD 11 (sustainable communities), SGD 13 (climate action), and SGD 14 (life below water).

Beyond the SDG-related impacts mentioned above, the ScIFC will also generate wider co-benefits in terms of which it is difficult to quantify at this stage. In terms of the broader economy, we know that energy, lighting and improved waste management practices enabled by projects provide the contexts necessary for the creation of new jobs and businesses. This can lead to increased income generation, poverty reduction, generating economic activity and employment.

For education, we know that access to energy and in particular electricity enables children to study at home after dark, which has been shown to improve educational results by lengthening study-time for children after dark, in addition to making adult professional education an option for many parents. Regarding public health, we know that appropriate waste management practices can reduce the spread of disease, while access to clean water and sanitation reduces exposure of the wider public to air pollutants and water pollutants and reducing the exposure of those living close to boreholes to faecal contamination. This has a positive impact on children’s health and/or debilitating effects (including for example malnutrition of children with mothers suffering from anaemia or electronic waste during pregnancy).

Access to clean, reliable, safe water is a multiplier of development co-benefits in addition to SDG-related impacts such as environmental benefits (germination, erosion), health and well-being improvements, and particular gender-related co-benefits since the majority of water retrieval in the developing world is done by women and girls. Gold Standard has certified water project impacts against SGD 1, no poverty; SGD 2, zero hunger; SGD 3, good health and well-being; SGD 5, gender equality; and SGD 6, Clean Water and Sanitation. Standard certified (gender responsive) water project, women and children saved more than 2 hours a day on water collection, which has allowed them to spend this time in education, income generation, and empowerment focused activities within the community. The project also measured—and approached gender parity, with its Water Resource Committees comprised of 46% female to 54% male. Since the project was implemented, there have been a 30% reduction in water-related incidents compared to 35% prior to the project. And borehole users have reported an 85% reduction in incidents of bullying, intimidation and assault during water collection since the borehole was rehabilitated.


---
**Page 54**
---

As for gender inclusive development, there is research showing that women and girls would benefit the most from access to improved energy services in developing countries. Due to their traditional responsibilities for collecting fuel and water, which is time-consuming and physically exhausting, women are often unable to participate fully in education, health care, or other income-generating activities. It is well known that in many developing countries, much of women's time is taken up with difficult and time-consuming tasks such as fetching water and wood, cooking over open fires, and cleaning clothes and in cooking with dirty fuels. A 2015 World Bank study, which focuses primarily on rural electrification, states that access to electricity can have a significant impact on the lives of women. The study found that improving access to security and gains women increased mobility at night, which could help female-led small businesses to operate in the evenings. Television could also help reduce violence, particularly against women, by providing an alternative to watching violent programs. Improved relations and giving women a greater understanding of their legal rights. In short, reduced drudgery for women can have dramatic effects on women's levels of empowerment, education, literacy, nutrition, health, economic opportunities, and involvement in community activities.

### D. Needs of recipient (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

Describe the scale and intensity of vulnerability of the country and beneficiary groups and elaborate how the project will address these needs (e.g., by targeting specific vulnerable groups or areas, etc.). Describe how the project/programme addresses the following needs:

- **Economic and social development level of the country and affected population**
- **Access to services and infrastructure (including the employment gap that prevents government from addressing the needs of the country. and lack of depth and history in the local capital market)**
- **Need for strengthening institutions and implementation capacity**

In general, least Developed Countries, Developing Countries and Countries in transition face a significant demand in terms of access to basic services such as health, education, energy, water and sanitation. The gap is largest amongst cities and regions is for energy, waste optimization and energy efficient city lighting services.

However, the low carbon, climate resilient infrastructure projects that must be implemented to meet this demand is far below the level that one might expect, primarily for two reasons:

Project Elements:
Projects are not being conceived, from the outset, to be "bankable" – i.e., projects that are:
- Properly structured in technical and financial terms (amongst other) using appropriate reliable technology, with a clear business case and a viable revenue stream serviced by reliable counterparties, appropriately insured and generating profits for investors;
- Conceived in full alignment with their operating context (amongst the other) local community and relevant stakeholders are involved in the development of national laws regulations and incentive schemes, and the international financial and political context


---
**Page 55**
---

The challenge lies with the "project development value chain" and the limited degree of collaboration, understanding and interconnection of the different social, economic, political and regulatory, and technology and finance players involved in project development and implementation. Additionally, key players do not fully understand how to make projects bankable or why they are not underwriting each other's risks and how their decisions can impact negatively on each other, and therefore on the bankability of projects.

## Access to Finance

The availability of appropriate financing instruments to fund the development and implementation of appropriate projects, at scale. If a country is being overwhelmed by green infrastructure projects of an intermediate size of between 5 and 75 MUSD, located near urban communities, and that can most easily be replicated by leveraging existing financial instruments, then there may not be sufficient demand for these projects to warrant green infrastructure in terms of number, aggregate investment opportunity and or potential for climate and development co-benefits.

For projects of this size however, there is a significant "funding gap", a lack of appropriate financial instruments to finance project identification development and implementation, that deliver upon the expected social, economic, environmental and climate benefits. This is because the project situation is distinct from that of smaller projects that have less difficulty in finding donor capital and local sources of funding, and from larger projects that tend to attract development finance investors and smaller institutional capital.

## The 100 campaign

The 100 campaign was strongly highlighted during the 100 Climate Solutions Projects Campaign undertaken by initially in 2016, with its latest update in 2019. Working through R20's networks of cities and regions - approaching 350 members worldwide - and in collaboration with national governments, international organizations, local/regional governments and private companies, the campaign consisted of an open call to submit potential projects for support. The accepted submissions included letters of mandate and support from municipal or regional authorities, as well as a project description and a business plan. These documents outline basic market data. The inaugural edition of the campaign identified more than 650 potential projects that were submitted in formal letters of mandate and political support.

## Alignment with NDCs

The SCiF prioritizes the integration of sub-national climate and sustainability priorities with those at a national level, including but not limited to National Determined Contributions (NDCs) and Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs). The SCiF will work closely with countries to ensure that there is alignment on this. First is engagement with the relevant federal line Ministries, and participation where possible with their established agencies or working groups already established to ensure alignment between sub-national priorities and national priorities. Second is the identification of key SDG-aligned delivery agencies regularly facilitate multi-level committees to identify, align, and flag any potential areas of priority for investment. Third is the development of a clear road map that outlines how SCiF support will be aligned and a clear reporting path between sub-national investment opportunities an national priorities. Fourth is the establishment of a network of country champions in each country to ensure country delivery agencies. SCiF will leverage this network in each host country to ensure contact is made and alignment with national priorities is achieved.

Gold Standard will play a particular supporting role as an implementing partner in the NDC-P. Through NDC-P, Gold Standard has access to government agencies and to the specific contacts responsible for establishing and managing NDCs. The SCiF will work closely with these agencies to ensure that each project must make a measurable contribution to climate (SDG 13 – Climate Action) and a minimum of two other SDGs (ex: SDG3 - Health and Well Being, SDG5 – Gender Equality, SDG6 - Clean Water and Sanitation, SDG7 - Affordable and Clean Energy). The SCiF will also ensure alignment with national level SDG and NDC reporting.

Projects are poorly designed and implemented

- Projects are designed by an equipment provider and not aligned to the exact needs of the territory.
- Participatory processes are often missing before project design is completed and local authorities' inputs are not considered.
- ESCOs are often hired after project design and leave few facilities for design improvements.
- Project fails to be effectively implemented.
- Construction, Operation and Maintenance is challenging

The TA allows better project design and seeks solutions adapted to the given territory.

Specific barriers to Energy Efficiency projects

- ESCO models are not always compliant with public sector regulations.
- ESCOs are not known in the public sector; complex financial mechanisms.
- Energy prices are often subsidised by nature. EE projects are often seen as a cost rather than an investment threshold.

The SnCF is working on the creation of adapted ESCO models and will support ECO users.

Specific barriers to Renewable Energy projects

- Lack of appropriate regulation
- Lack of clarity for IPP schemes
- Lack of bankable off-takers

The deal structure score adopted to these types of projects

- The TA can support the authorities in designing a framework for and negotiate appropriate PPAs.
- The counterparty risk is partially covered by SnCF in its capital structure of SnCF.

Project "smaller" size reduces the financial needs and specific situation can be analysed in the project.

Specific barriers to Waste Optimisation projects

- Lack of financial resources of municipalities
- Low awareness in the sector
- Existing informal sector
- High volumes of waste for traditional waste infrastructure in more "rural"
- Decentralised infrastructure can lower costs is less adapted to generate value out of the recovered/reduced material rather than paying to get rid of it.

### Implementation

Every SGP project will be undertaken with an official mandate from the relevant subnational authorities, and in close collaboration with country-level authorities. Ensuring such alignment is a key element of project selection and engagement. Specific focus on National Development Strategies (NDS), national targets for Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs), NAMAs, prevailing gender policies, international commitments, etc., will be considered during the project design phase. SGP will also engage in contacts with other relevant Ministries related to national NDCs. Similarly, with 55 country offices, SGP has strong links and a presence at the subnational level. If a country office is not yet established when an SGP project receives an NOL, GCF will be consulted to ensure engagement and alignment with other GCF country programmes.

Country priorities will be reflected in the work of the regional Project Accelerator Facilities. The facility training programs will target sub-national stakeholders but will also provide training for regional and

GCF participation goes well beyond only increasing the ROI (return on investment) of the Fund as its focus is first and foremost to deliver climate mitigation and adaptation benefits. In addition, it will be important for the project/programme to have a strong governance structure with an active steering committee/meeting, to ensure the project is managed in ways that continue to improve its efficiency and effectiveness throughout its implementation. Part of Component 3 on development and use of methodologies and tools will include support for developing and testing new subproject designs and also a key criteria for monitoring progress. # E Monitoring, reporting and evaluation arrangements (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

The monitoring, reporting and evaluation of the Technical Assistance project is as follows:

## Project level monitoring and evaluation will be undertaken in compliance with the IUCN 2015 Monitoring and Evaluation Policy, the Accreditation Master Agreement (AMA) between CCF and S4CF and the IUCN Access to Information Policy.

### The monitoring process will measure achievement of the performance indicators and report on the implementation progress against the project baseline. It will be conducted in accordance with the principles established in the GCF Initial Results Management Framework time-bound sets of activities as a set of key performance indicators (KPIs) that are aligned to a standard set of internationally recognized indicators aligned to its donors core indicators, including GCF, S4CF and IUCN. The standardized indicators for each project will be used as measurement tools to ensure effective data collection and harmonized reporting.

### A project inception workshop will be held after the project document is signed by all relevant parties to: a) re-orient project stakeholders to the project strategy and discuss any changes in the overall context that influence project implementation; b) review the project baseline, including the project’s assumptions, risk mitigation lines and conflict resolution mechanisms; c) review the results framework, re-assess baselines as needed, and discuss reporting, monitoring and evaluation roles and responsibilities and finalize the M&E plan; d) review financial


---
**Page 70**
---

reporting procedures and mandatory requirements, and agrees on the arrangements for the annual audit, ex post evaluations, and schedule SnCF Steering Committee meetings and finalise the first year annual work plan. R20 will prepare the implementation plan later than one month after the inception workshop. A final inception report will be submitted to the GCF Secretariat.

Project mid-term (MTE) and final (FE) evaluations will be commissioned and managed by the SnCF Planning, Monitoring, Evaluation and Risk Unit – IUCN’s independent evaluation office. Independent evaluators under the oversight of the SnCF Steering Committee will conduct the MTE and FE evaluations in accordance with the GCF IEU Evaluation Criteria. The MTE will assess (1) the relevance, effectiveness, efficiency and likelihood of impact and sustainability of the project; (2) the extent to which the project has been implemented according to the terms of country ownership. The FE will assess (1) the relevance, effectiveness, efficiency and likelihood of impact and sustainability of the project; (2) the extent to which the project has been implemented according to the terms of country ownership; (3) and innovation in result areas; and (4) replication and scalability. The evaluation will apply a pre-determined set of criteria for each of these dimensions, with specific indicators and performance measures. The IUCN management response will be reviewed by the SnCF Steering Committee. Evaluation reports and monitoring results will be shared with the GCF Secretariat.

IUCN will retain all M&E records for this project for up to six years after project financial closure in order to support ex-post evaluations. A detailed M&E budget, monitoring plan and evaluation plan will be included in the proposal and ESMS framework.

The Executive Entities, which are R20, Gold Standard and IUCN, will be responsible to lead the presentations of the project’s outputs and activities they are responsible for. Under the facilitation of the EE the contracted organizations for each sub-activities (that are in line with the project TOC and the budget) will be presented the proposals. Any technical risks are exposed and discussed at the SnCF Steering Committee.

Mitigation Measure(s)

The technological risk mitigation is key in the Fund Technical Assistance design. Selected Risk Factor: Operations not performing as planned

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |

Description

Please describe the risk to the best of your knowledge at this point in time.

Operations are critical for the smooth running of the projects. Poor operation management, employees' qualifications, knowledge transfer can lead to low performance reducing incomes and misconducts against the bidding process.

Mitigation Measure(s)

Please describe how the identified risk will be mitigated or managed. Do the mitigation measures lower the probability of risk occurring? If so, to what level?

In its Due Diligence, the Fund will assess the operation teams and, when applicable, promote contracting an OM partner. The Technical Assistance will assist the project sponsor in the procurement process and the selection of a suitable OM partner. Furthermore, the Fund's legal team will ensure that OM contracts have proper clauses for financial compensations covering operational risks.

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |

Description

Please describe the risk to the best of your knowledge at this point in time.

Given the geographical scope of the Fund, many projects will be generated in various local currencies. Incomes will subsequently be affected by different exchange rates.

Mitigation Measure(s)

Please describe how the identified risk will be mitigated or managed. Do the mitigation measures lower the probability of risk occurring? If so, to what level?

The Fund is promoting sources of income in hard currencies for its assets under management. The Fund strictly avoids entering into debt agreements signed in USD. Moreover currency risks are evaluated for each project in the portfolio and it estimated to high, specific mitigation insurances will be subscribed by the Fund.

## Selected Risk Factor: Population acceptance of the projects

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Other | Low | Description |

Please describe the risk to the best of your knowledge at this point in time.

The primary environmental and social risks faced by the Fund is associated with the degree of acceptance and support from local communities. This can be a major barrier, for example, should a solar PV project that may connect to the national electricity grid, not also meet the needs of a local population. Should such projects fail to gain sufficient acceptance from the local community (undermining the continuation of the project) or even financially failure of the project (undermining the sustainability of the overall Programme).

Mitigation Measure(s)

Please describe how the identified risk will be mitigated or managed. Do the mitigation measures lower the probability of risk occurring? If so, to what level?

The GCF Global will focus on "inclusive" sustainable projects building upon a bottom-up approach where communities at sub-national level are the initiators of the projects. The Programme further mitigates such risks by integrating sustainable impact criteria into the entire value chain from project conception, through implementation and monitoring, to final evaluation. A new integrated sustainability measurement, reporting and verification tool developed in partnership with the Gold Standard foundations.

The full set of procedures that will be followed includes a process for screening of potential investments against the Decision List prior to a Go/No-Go Decsion to ensure that no investment is made in projects or companies that are operating with excluded activities. The screening process provides the provincial committee with information on the project’s gender equality and women’s empowerment (GEWE) considerations, and then determines the level of EHS due diligence required and the actions to be taken to minimize potential impacts. G.2. Gender assessment and action plan (max 500 words, approximately 1 page)

Provide a summary of the project design and projections/prime-level gender action plan that is aligned with the SNCF Gender Policy. Consider including tables or charts in this section describing the process used to develop both documents. Provide information on the key findings re: vulnerability and why key resources are needed. Describe how the gender analysis was integrated into the project design. Describe consultations that have taken place and describe the key insights integrated into the action plan, including: how addressing the GEWE considerations will be reflected in the project’s design, implementation, monitoring, evaluation, and reporting. Describe the expected outcomes of the project/programme with targets; implementation arrangements where the AE has put in place to ensure activities are implemented and expected outcomes will be achieved monitored and evaluated.

Provide the full gender assessment and project-level gender action plan as annex 6.


The main objective of the SNCF Gender Policy is to strengthen the project’s responsiveness to the multiple, often intersecting, dimensions of gender equality. The policy aims at ensuring that projects address both (a) the links between gender equality, natural resources management and environmental sustainability. Global literature makes clear that women’s participation in design, distribution, management and production of energy resources is critical for sustainable development and poverty reduction; and (b) considers as indispensable to realising the climate change targets as well as sustainable development goals.


The SNCF Gender Policy applies to the fund itself and to all projects participating in the SNCF. The policy outlines the principles for achieving gender responsiveness, and through supporting technical guidance on how to integrate gender considerations into project design, implementation, monitoring, evaluation, and implementation of projects. Gender mainstreaming will be considered in all stages of the project cycle (design, formulation, implementation, monitoring, and evaluation) by integrating ‘gender mainstreaming’ as a principle within the SNCF’s Environmental and Social Management Information System (ESMS)). This includes the following:

- Gender questions in the ES&A screening checklists in initial project screening stage, and in monitoring forms.
- Inclusion of a 'gender assessment' part of ES&A (Environmental & Social Impact Assessment) for early projects.
- Use of gender-specific stakeholder consultation requirements and safeguards, based on Gold Standard methodology. The ES&A is designed to be used as a tool designed for project implementation; the requirements form the body of this policy document.


The SNCF will put in place processes to ensure that projects align with prevailing national gender strategy (if host country has one) or other public policy for gender equality and women’s empowerment, and will seek to integrate these into the ES&A. The ES&A will be used as a tool designed to identify risks in the intervention region or the sector. The policy incorporates elements of several gender frameworks (see the SNCF Gender Policy Framework). The policy also draws on the CEDAW, which incorporates many elements from The Committee on the Elimination of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW).

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.1 | PAGE 73 OF 77

## Gender and social outputs
### SnCF Targets
- **Gender and social inclusion policy at fund level**: Endorsement of gender and social inclusion policy by fund management committee.
- **Gender procedures and checklists incorporated in the fund's Environmental and Social Framework (ESF)**: Comprehensive ESFs with updated gender procedures.
- **Engagement and consultation of women in all stages of project cycle**: At least 40% representation of women in all stakeholder consultations, with separate meetings for women if necessary.
- **Recruitment of women in projects**: At least 20% of employees will be female.
- **Creation of opportunities to enhance women's empowerment initiatives**: At least 10% of project costs will be allocated to women's empowerment initiative (e.g., women attending skills-based training programme related to project livelihood).
- **Recruitment of women in monitoring teams**: At least 20% of project monitoring teams will be female.

## Definitions*
### Term
**Agency**: The capacity to make decisions about one’s own life and act on them to achieve a desired outcome, free of violence, retribution, or fear.
**Empowerment**: The process by which women gain control over their lives. It includes: (1) the ability to make choices; (2) the sense of self-worth; (3) the right to have and to determine choices; (4) the right to be heard in decision-making processes, including those that affect her life; (5) the ability to influence others’ lives. Empowerment is a process that can occur at different levels: individual, family, community, national, and internationally.
**Gender**: Refers to the socially constructed, cultural, and attitudinal characteristics, expectations, and norms associated with being male or female.
**Gender equality**: The principle of equal rights, opportunities, responsibilities, and obligations for women and men. It is achieved when there are no gender-based differences in access to resources, opportunities, and decision-making power.
**Gender equity**: Refers to the process of being fair to women and men. To ensure equity, it is necessary to address the historical and social disadvantages that prevent women and men from otherwise operating on an equal footing.
**Gender responsive**: Refers to the consideration of gender norms, roles and relations and addressing inequalities generated by these norms in all aspects of planning and implementing action creating gender awareness.
**Gender sensitive**: Refers to the process of improving the quality of gender norms, roles and relations but does not necessarily address inequality generated by unusual norms, roles or relations.
**Social inclusion**: Refers to the process of improving the rights for individuals and groups to take part in society, and the process of improving the mobility, opportunity, and dignity of those marginalized on the basis of their identity to take part in society (World Bank).

*If not otherwise indicated, relevant definitions are drawn and adapted from the GCF gender policy or the Annex to the GEF Gender Equality Action Plan (GEAP).

# G3. Financial management and procurement (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

Describe the project/programme's financial management including the financial monitoring systems, financial accounting, budgeting, and disbursement structure and methods. Refer to section 8.4 on implementation arrangements as necessary.

Articulate any procurement issues that may require attention, e.g., procurement implementation arrangements and the role of the implementing agency (IA) in managing procurements. Indicate whether procurements will be managed by the AE or the implementing agency. Provide a detailed procurement plan as annex 10.

## The financial management and procurement framework is as follows:

1. The project's financial management will follow the Technical Assistance guide to IUCN's internal financial rules and regulations which are part of the IUCN Finance Manual. In situations where there may be differences between the TA guide and the IUCN Finance Manual, the latter shall prevail.
2. The procurement aspects of this project will be guided by IUCN’s “Policy and Procedure on Procurement of Goods and Services” (https://www.iucn.org/procedures), that is a standard professional procurement policy and elaborate standards for conducting procurements in an efficient and cost-effective manner while respecting sustainability, the environment and efficiency.
3. The project will be audited in accordance with the International Standards on Auditing (ISA). This work form part of the annual statutory audit undertaken by IUCN’s corporate auditor, Price Waterhouse Coopers. In terms of the procurement process, the auditors will review all relevant documentation related to the procurement process and the auditors and the audit will be scheduled around end of the project and conducted in line with ISA. To supplement these audits, periodic financial reviews will be conducted as part of regular monitoring.

We refer to the Global Sub-national Climate Fund (ScF Global) Programme and the Technical Assistance Facility for ScF Global formed by the two Funding Proposals submitted by the Pegasus Capital Advisors and the International Union for Conservation of Nature, respectively, to us on 23 April 2020 that are collectively referred to as the "Programme".

The undersigned is the duly authorized representative of the Ministry of Economy, the National Designated Authority (NDA) for Fiji.

Pursuant to GCF decision 08/10, the content of which we acknowledge to have reviewed, we hereby communicate our no objection to the Programme as included in the two Funding Proposals.

By communicating our no-objection, it is implied that:

(a) The Fijian Government has no objection to the Programme as included in the two Funding Proposals;

(b) The Programme as included in the two Funding Proposal is in conformity with Fiji's national priorities, strategies and plans; and

(c) In accordance with relevant environmental and social safeguards, the Programme as included in the two Funding Proposals is in conformity with relevant national laws and regulations.

We confirm that our national process for accounting no objection to the Programme, as included in the two Funding Proposals, have been duly followed. We also confirm that our no objection applies to all projects or activities to be implemented within the scope of the Programme and that this letter will be made publicly available on the GCF website.

Thank you

Malakere Korobe
Permanent Secretary for Economy

КАБИНЕТ НА ЗАМЕНИЦИТА НА ПРЕДСЕДАТЕЛJОТ НА ВЛАДАТА ЗАДОЛЖЕНА ЗА ЕКОНОМИЧКИ ПРАВАЊЕ И КООРДИНАЦИЈА СО ЕКОНОМИЧКите РЕСПОРИ

17 - 05 - 2020

Архивски броj: 09-4603 / 7

До: Зелени климатски фонд

Предмет: Писмо без приговор за Предлог проект на формирање на регионални експерт каунтсфод фонд и Технички помош за восстапување на Фондот преку Зелениот климатски фонд поднесен од Peguma Capital Advisers и Метрополна униса за зачувување на природата.

Почнување:

Повикнувајќи се на Предлог проектите за формирање на регионални експерт каунтсфод фонд и Технички помош за восстапување на Фондот преку Зелениот климатски фонд поднесени од Peguma Capital Advisers и Метрополна униса за зачувување на природата, до Национално назначените орган на Република Северна Македонија, г.о. Мила Шороска, Заменик-министер на Владата на Република Северна Македонија за економски прашања.

Во согласност со одлуката на Зелениот климатски фонд бр. ВБ/10, со чија содржина сме запознани, го доставувааме ова писмо без приговор за проервата како што е назначено во датот предлог проект.

Со доставувањето на овоа писмо без приговор, се известиувам дека:

04


---
**Page 128**
---

Влада на Република Северна Македонија

ЗАМЕНИЧКА НА ПРЕСТДАТЕЛНАТА ВЛАДА НА РЕПУБЛИКА СЕВЕРНА МАКЕДОНИЈА ЗА ЕКОНОМИСКИ ПРОГРАМ 2017

a) Владата на Република Северна Македонија нема забележана по однос на предходниот период, ако не е наведено во датата предлог-проект.

b) Програвано како што е наведено во дата предлог-проект и е во соразмерност со националните приоритети, стратегии и планови.

c) Во соразмерност со намерите за залагање на предпоставки и содржинските резултати на Земенскиот каминашки фонд, програвано како што е наведено во дата предлог-проект и е во соразмерност со координирани национални камини за резултати.

Исто така, потврдување дека националниот процес за поддржување на писмо без приговор за програваната како што е наведено во дата предлог-проект е споредено.

Воедно, не изнесување дека писмото без приговор се однесува на сите активности кои не бидат спроведени во рамките на предлог-проектите.

Се согласуваме како што е наведено да биде чинеодело преку себ стравата на Земенскиот каминашки фонд.

Ва благодарност за соработката.

Со повик,

ЗАМЕНИЧКА НА ПРЕСТДАТЕЛНАТА ВЛАДА НА РЕПУБЛИКА СЕВЕРНА МАКЕДОНИЈА ЗА ЕКОНОМИСКИ ПРОГРАМ 2017

(Инициативно подписано)

We also confirm that our no objection has been duly followed.

We also confirm that our no objection applies to all projects or activities to be implemented within the scope of the Programme.

# Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment of FP151

## Proposal name: Global Sub-national Climate Fund (SoCF Global) – Equity; Technical Assistance (TA) Facility for the Global Subnational Climate Fund (GSCF)

### Accredited entities:
- Pegasus Capital Advisors LP (Pegasus)
- International Union for Conservation of Nature and Natural Resources (IUCN)

### Project/programme size: Medium and Small

## Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel

### 1.1 Impact potential Scale: Uncertain

#### 1. This assessment considers the medium-scale programme submitted to GCF through two independent funding proposals by two accredited independent entities (AEs). The programme has received a positive recommendation from the Independent Technical Advisory Panel (ITAP) of Pegasus Capital Advisors LP (Pegasus) in the AoT to GCF, and a technical assistance (TA) component (grant), for which the IUCN is the implementing entity. The International Union for Conservation of Nature and Natural Resources (IUCN) is the AE to GCF. Conceptually, the equity component is the main element of the entire programme, and the grant component is a supplementary component mainly to ensure TA support for the implementation of the equity component.

#### 2. The programme will be implemented through two independent funded agreements: one between Pegasus and GCF and another between IUCN and GCF. The reason for this implementation arrangement is the accreditation agreement between GCF and these two AEs. As clarified in the ITAP report, Pegasus Capital Advisors LP (Pegasus) is accredited for the management of equity-related funding proposals but is not authorized for the management of non-equity related funding proposals. Similarly, IUCN is accredited for the management of non-grant proceeds from GCF. Accordingly, the proposed arrangement was developed to ensure that both entities are able to manage their respective components within this AE.

#### 3. The programme will be overseen and implemented by a consortium of GCF AEs each with diverse expertise and a long track record of operating sources:
- (a) Pegasus: a GCF AE, will oversee management of SoCF Global and investment of the commitments of SoCF Global in individual projects.
- (b) IUCN – Regions of Action (ROA) [2021] will be an advisor to SoCF Global, and having extensive experience partnering with subnational authorities in the field of low-carbon and


---
**Page 144**
---

climate-resilient infrastructure projects. R20 will manage the project implementing unit of the TA component:

(b) Gold Standard® will advise SnCF Global on monitoring and reporting tools and metrics on climate change mitigation and adaptation impact, environmental and social management systems, and Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs). In addition, Gold Standard will ensure establishment of all necessary procedures for third-party certification of projects under the terms of climate mitigation, adaptation, ecosystem integrity, local rights, gender, and the SDGs.

(c) BNP, a GCF AE, will oversee and implement a TA facility established by a separate GCF grant (through a separate funding proposal). The TA facility is intended to facilitate a robust implementation of the project.

4. SnCF Global will be established by the equity component of the programme submitted through a medium-sized funding proposal. It will consist of GCF equity at 20 per cent ($150 million), with the balance of the equity share (80 per cent) leveraged from public and private sources. Of this, $460 million (80 per cent ($360 million, equity)) will be leveraged by SnCF Global from private (80 per cent share) and public (20 per cent share) sources, with the balance of the equity share to be provided by GCF. The public share will be about 44 per cent, and the remaining 34 per cent will be public money, including the GCF contribution. If the project is successful in achieving its climate change mitigation impact and economic returns as well, the programme aims to overcome the project-level barriers that lead to chronic underfunding of bankable mitigation projects at the subnational level. The equity share will be invested by Pegasus in SnCF Global through the process of GCF funding proposals. The AIs believe that anchor funding from GCF will unlock both public inventory (e.g., development banks, multilateral agencies, national development banks, etc.) and even more importantly, private inventories (pension funds, insurance funds, family organizations (NGOs), and academic and financial institutions). Its mission is to accelerate sustainable infrastructure investments in the green economy to meaningfully contribute to the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs).

Gold Standard® will ensure that projects under SnCF Global are designed with high carbon emissions features that deliver the highest levels of environmental integrity and also contribute to sustainable development. The Gold Standard® has a long history of working on climate change mitigation practice and supporting developing interventions. See <https://www.goldstandard.org/about-acquisition-and-mission>.

The GCF investment in SnCF Global will be held by a newly formed holding company managed by Pegasus on behalf of GCF. The equity share will be invested by Pegasus in SnCF Global through the process to SnCF Global for the terms of the funding activity agreement. For details, see the structure chart in annex 29 and Annex 30.

The general partner (to be determined by Pegasus) and its affiliates, partners and employees will commit to investing at least $15 million each year into total commitments of SnCF Global. For details, see the Pegasus commitment in the term sheet in annex 14.


---
**Page 145**
---

offices, private banks, philanthropies, high net worth individuals, and other institutional investors) that are willing to invest in green finance products but lack access to investment vehicles to invest in it at the subnational level. The AE (PCA) plans to mobilize public (20 per cent) and private (80 per cent) resources for the implementation of projects under GCF, at scale to de-risk substantial, mid-scale, infrastructure projects. The programme plans to cover all green energy sectors.

- For each individual project, ScNf Global will make equity investments into special purpose vehicles that manage the project. Each of the individual subnational projects in which ScNf Global invests will be structured as a separate legal entity. In total, 40 per cent share will be from leveraged private and public investments; a 4 per cent share will be from GCF (USD 15 million); and 56 per cent of the equity will come from ScNf Global. The remaining 8 per cent should be co-financing from the project as equity. According to this funding proposal, such a partnership would enable the programme to leverage the public development finance, which on its own is not enough to achieve the Paris Agreement objective and the significant scaling up of investment from the private sector is needed. According to this general structure, USD 10 from GCF will leverage USD 40.

- The equity funding proposal has four EEs:
(a) Pegasus, a limited partnership registered in Delaware, the United States of America;
(b) ScNf Global, not yet formed but expected to be an investment company in risk capital (investment funds) and private equity (venture capital) with its headquarters in Luxembourg with a designated alternative investment fund manager; 
(c) R20, an association based in Geneva, Switzerland, under Swiss association law; and
(d) Gold Standard, an association registered in Brussels, Belgium, under Belgian association law.

- The TA component of the programme is to establish the pipeline of national-level climate change mitigation investments projects for further financing from the equity component, ScNf Global. An additional function of this component is to develop special tools/protocols and methodologies that will be used by the project developers in order to ensure a more efficient and effective selection process for different sectors and different activities at the project and fund level. These tools will be used by the programme to identify projects that are most likely to have significant climate change mitigation results, and contribution to GCF targets. Another activity to be performed by the TA component is of strengthening the capacity of the relevant stakeholders (project developers) in order to ensure a more efficient and effective selection process for their participation in their respective projects' outreach activities, thus further promoting replicability and scalability.

- The TA component of the programme is a small-scale funding proposal with a total cost of USD 10 million - with USD 9.83 million from GCF, and USD 0.17 mln co-financing, with a significant portion of this funding to be used for capacity building.

- The TA funding proposal has three EEs: IBCN, R20 and Gold Standard.

- The programme implementation period is 15 years, with an estimated whole-programme lifetime cost of USD 3.8 billion. The total cost of the equity funding proposal is 3,881,722 €/GCF annually, and 77,649,432 €/GCF in total for the 20 years of the programme's lifespan.

- An independent evaluation of projects and programmes under this programme may be adapted or benefits of invested GCF mitigation projects are anticipated and will be monitored. As an independent evaluation project, a programme under this programme may have any impact on the climate change adaptation but is crucial for successful implementation of the programme.


---
**Page 146**
---

13. TAP considers that targeting currently underfunded subnational level projects is an important activity and supports this sector being a focus area for GCF. However, based on the information provided, TAP is not in position to assess and confirm the final impact of the programme on this sector.
14. The programme is submitted through two independent funding proposals: one is responsible for the TA component, and the other is responsible for the investment component. TAP notes that the proposed project activities are consistent with the fact that the proposed structure contradicts the guidelines that the draft decision contemplates as effective in addressing climate change. TAP also notes that there is no assessment in the section on efficiency and effectiveness. The opinion of TAP is that, with such an implementation arrangement, the level of risk is high and the anticipated impact may not be high.
(b) The programme does not have any legal regulatory mechanism defining the responsibilities of the parties involved (e.g., GCF, host country government, implementing partner, etc.)
(c) Because the fund, at this stage, has been submitted as a “blind pool”, the GHG mitigation potential is estimated through theoretical funding proposals by sector; and
(d) The remaining impediments are highlighted throughout the assessment in different sections.

### 1.2 Paradigm-shift potential Scale: High

15. The concept of the programme should be considered as decentralization of the GCF investment model to subnational level (i.e., GCF Global equity), which could leverage additional private and public capital (according to the funding proposal, USD 3 from GCF will leverage USD 20) and thus increase the scale of projects that can be supported. TAP notes that, even considering that substantial authorities/mayors in many developing countries worldwide have announced their intention to participate in the programme, there is no clear indication of their authority. Such an approach, targeting subnational projects worldwide, could accelerate the removal of the existing barriers for project promoters and facilitate the scaling up of underfunded high-priority activities at the subnational level by leveraging sector maximum de-risking mechanisms with public money.
16. GCF Global equity can be funded through proposals from private investors (Asian Development Bank, European Bank for Reconstruction and Development, etc.) targeting subnational infrastructure projects. However, the added value of GCF Global is to increase the participation of developing countries in the programme by providing a guarantee of the results achieved.
17. The component of the programme will contribute to making subnational processes more proactive, which is one of the key barriers in the underfunding of these projects.

### 1.3 Sustainable development potential Scale: High

18. In the event of successful implementation of the programme, the contribution of subnational level (water supply and sanitation, street lighting, renewable energy, energy efficiency, etc.) to the sustainable development of regions will be significant, and this will be strengthened monitoring and certification results.


---
**Page 147**
---

19. Ensuring and strengthening the sustainable development potential of the programme and SaCf Global is the direct responsibility of the TA component of the programme, which should be implemented through a separate funding proposal led by IUCN as the AE. In particular, the TA component will be responsible for developing the MRF system and for establishing the third-party certification process for the projects under the programme.

20. In particular, Gold Standard, which is an EE in both funding proposals (TA and SaCf Global), is responsible for the development of sector-related general monitoring tools and indicators as well as project-specific monitoring tools and indicators related to climate change related SDGs. Moreover, the same will be done for each project taking into consideration the project's specific context and needs. The TA component will also be responsible for national projects as criteria for investment deal identification, selection and, later, for monitoring of results. According to existing programming strategies metrics will apply: “no harm” and actively promoting sustainable development.

21. In addition to climate change mitigation and adaptation co-benefits (SDG 13), these subnational projects should contribute to several SDGs in particular, the sample projects provided by IUCN are:

* **SDG 3 - good health and well-being:** Results from the implementation of waste and water management projects will reduce exposure to hazardous chemicals and those from air, water and soil pollution and contamination.
* **SDG 7 - affordable and clean energy:** Planning investments in renewable energy and energy efficiency measures will contribute to increasing the share of renewable energy in the global energy mix, improve the global rate of energy efficiency, and promote investment in energy infrastructure and clean energy resources.
* **SDG 11 - sustainable cities and communities:** Subnational projects could enhance inclusive and sustainable urbanization, increase the capacity of local governments to manage urban development, reduce the adverse impact of human settlement, and reduce the adverse per capita environmental impact of cities, including by paying special attention to air quality and municipal and other waste management.
* **Further SDGs:** Some subnational projects that are not dedicated to the MRF in terms of climate change, SDG and nature-based solutions.

### 1.4 Needs of the recipient

Scale: High

23. Based on its experience and knowledge of barriers to the implementation of subnational projects, TAP confirms that the needs of developing countries to activate underfinanced subnational projects are very high. Despite formally decentralized processes, subnational governments in many developing countries lack sufficient capacity for financial and regulatory support of national/central governments, and so have studied in many developing countries. The specific context of each country, including its political situation, region-specific barriers and potential projects are crucial. However, a consultation process with stakeholders is necessary to effectively identify and define the funding proposal or the no objection letters (NOLs) submitted.

25. The alignment of this programme with nationally determined contributions (NDCs) is highly desirable as it will be included in paragraph 24 to the functional scope. TAP fully recognizes the importance of intended nationally determined contributions (INDCs) in climate change decision-making process. However, NDCs are more national or federal strategies, which


---
**Page 148**
---

not sufficiently relevant and comprehensive enough for investors to analyze countries' needs and barriers to climate change investments at the subnational, sectoral and project level. For such a detailed assessment of the alignment of activities with NDCs, the implementation plans should be used. These are currently not available or are not yet ready in most countries.

25. The non-bankability issue is mostly limited-access to credit on small scale and decentralized projects were identified as key barriers to investing in subnational projects In the first submission of the funding proposal for B2G, TAP expressed concerns that, without a preliminary assessment, it would be impossible to determine the needs of the recipients. During the assessment process, TAP shared its concern with the GCF team and the World Bank. The GCF team agreed that it was possible to overcome this barrier through the TA component; however, there are several other barriers that prevent projects from becoming bankable (affordability for the population, end-user willingness to pay, etc.) that cannot be resolved at the national level. In response to B27 submission, the GCF conducted a more in-depth analysis of key sectors that SoGC Global would plan to consider investing in 40 countries (in 4 regions).

26. Some of the key barriers identified at this stage of the preliminary assessment report, reported by the AUS, are listed below: TAP recognizes that some of these barriers exist not only as the subnational level but also at the national level.

(a) Transaction costs for relatively small subnational projects are critical, and high transaction costs can be a significant barrier to bankability. The programme targets this barrier through the TA component;

(b) In most cases, projects are designed by an equipment provider and not always adapted to the local context. This is a key barrier that needs to be overcome under bankability. The programme targets this barrier through the TA component;

(c) Participatory processes are often performed after the project design has been completed. As such, it can be difficult for the programme to ensure that these processes will be conducted by the TA component for the projects in which it is decided to invest.

(d) Many grant programs and other public-private partnership initiatives do not usually fund projects with a cost greater than USD 5 million. SoGC Global will be limited to investing in subnational projects with a total cost of less than USD 5 million or USD 7.5 million;

(e) Many project developers struggle to find equity partners, and potential fund providers require repayment within two years due to the high risk associated with this barrier overcoming this barrier;

(f) Regarding renewable energy: key barriers identified are lack of appropriate regulation, lack of access to financing for power producer schemes, and lack of banking/off-takers.

(g) For the municipal waste management sector, key barriers identified are: lack of financial resources in local/municipal budgets; absence of regulations; existing informal sector; scattering of projects across large areas; lack of infrastructure (e.g., landfills); and lack of "rural" areas; and transportation costs that are too great to be supported by traditional business models.

(h) For energy efficiency projects and the energy service company approach, key barriers identified: are energy service company models are not always compliant with public


---
**Page 149**
---

1.5 Country ownership Scale: Uncertain

TAP not able to assess the country ownership of this programme due to the following reasons:

(a) Actual participant countries have not been identified at this stage, and the country allocations provided in an annex are theoretical. Regional-level preliminary allocations are proposed for each region.

(b) The programme applies the "blind pool" concept, which means that final recipient countries, sectors and particular projects are not selected at the stage of programme submission. Therefore, TAP has no way to assess country ownership.

(c) TAP has obtained information from NOIs provided by 40 countries here. However, TAP has also received NOIs from 12 countries in the Caribbean, Mediterranean and Central Asia regions that have not been included above.

(d) The observation by TAP is that the format of the NOI is fully standardized at the level of three or four obligatory requirements, and the country's attitude to the project or opinion on its inclusion in the programme is not expressed. This attitude may be expressed only in very exceptional cases. After reviewing all 40 of these NOIs, TAP did not find any indication that the countries would use the financing from this fund as equity such as see any significant benefit for their own countries. Moreover, this programme does not provide to countries' programmes and not registered in the AEC working programme.

(e) The maximum number of projects that could be financed by SoCG Global is 35, and of these, 20 are already committed to being funded from SoCG Global (due to uncertainty and limitations imposed by market, sponsors, timing, etc.). Therefore, TAP cannot assess country ownership for all countries. All countries will receive TA financing to build their capacity and improve the market for future investments.

At this stage of assessment, TAP could not assess country ownership. However, according to the criteria of SoCG Global for project selection is the request for a supporting letter from subnational authorities in case of any uncertainty regarding country ownership. It is the opinion of TAP that SoCG Global should request letters from both national and subnational authorities for the implementation of each particular project.

1.6 Efficiency and effectiveness

The efficiency and effectiveness of this programme depend significantly on the implementation arrangements. As described in the section on input potential, the programme is implemented through a series of agreements with the respective subnational AEs. Such an implementation modality, with unclear legal responsibilities of these AEs to all others and the absence of any formal agreement between the AE and the implementing partner, raises concerns on the potential risk of failure of the programme in the event of low liability or not enough experience on the part of one of the partners of the AE. As this implementation


---
**Page 150**
---

arrangements would be the first of its kind in GCF practice (at least, as known to TAP). TAP decided to make a review of the available Board policy documents and decisions in order to make an appropriate decision.

### n. TAP carefully reviewed all options offered either to the Board or by the Secretariat in the document titled “Policy on programmatic approaches”, which is being posted (not yet deliberated) by the Board). TAP found that, for all options considered within this policy paper, there is always one entity that would be responsible for the overall management of the project or programme. In a multiple-accredited entities modality, the document offers the consideration that:

- under this modality while there will be separate project-related roles and responsibilities among the accredited entities, there may also be a coordinating AE to oversee the project or programme; there is also here a for a coordinating AE to be responsible for the overall management of the project or programme. This would mean that the AE would have a dual role in handling reporting on the project or programme progress, results and learning.

Taking into consideration the options provided within the aforementioned document, including the one proposed by the Secretariat, TAP concludes that the proposed implementation modality is high risk. This is because the two proposals are not fully satisfying all elements of the GCF policy on programmatic approaches that could be considered by the Board in future.

The above-mentioned document also formulated criteria which should be considered for a GCF programme to be considered as a programmatic approach against these elements and concludes that the programme satisfies most of elements, but that two elements are still not fully satisfied:

- Programmes should be country-driven through observance of NQI procedures and alignment with national priorities. The programme has NQIs from 40 countries. However, it is noted that the GCF Secretariat is currently supporting multi-country programmes where countries do not see the particular sector and project that was proposed as a priority (this is more in line with the concept of “programme ownership”). It is the opinion of TAP that, in addition to this general NQI supporting the establishment of a country-driven programme officer for each individual project should be provided by countries at this late stage; and
- Programmatic approaches modality should be developed to foster collaboration among multiple entities. The document also states that the proposed implementation modalities should address issues that are likely to impede outcomes, performance, efficiency and effectiveness. In this regard, TAP notes that there is no need for a coordinating role for multi-agent initiatives. The programme as submitted satisfies the first part of these requirements but not the requirement on coordination (one AE being required).

In initial submission to B.26, the concept of SCGF Global was presented as a “blind pool” without any specific criteria or selection process and with no indication that it would be based on the basis of theoretical projects. At that time, the programme was quite unclear and planned to cover all results areas of GCF programmes without preliminary assessment of market demand, which is not in line with the policy on programmatic approaches for GCF. TAP has taken into consideration the fact that this concept of “blind pool” is new and has a high risk (including the risk of non-competitive bidding). In light of these considerations, TAP has decided to not recommend this programme to the Board for approval, also having in mind the elements of the programmatic approach from the document “Policy on programmatic approaches”.

In this submission to B.27, the “pool” is not fully “blind”; the AIs have provided demand analysis of subnational projects’ markets and relevantly revised priority results areas. It was


---
**Page 151**
---

found by the AEs that market demand at the subnational level is not equal for all GCF result areas. Thus, the previous approach of considering "all results area" was revised according to the consider those results areas where demand for equity investment is clear in its resolution. The TA component will be implemented through a Technical Assistance consortium, as listed above. Adaptation co-benefits of mitigation projects and contribution to the SBGs will be monitored by the AEs. This approach has been developed based on the experience from the barrier analysis conducted by the AEs demonstrates to the programme management the barriers that make this option unattractive for private investors. The main reason is the limited in required investments because of the medium or small size of subnational projects is not the only barrier that makes this sector unattractive for private investors. These studies have contributed to the development of a new approach, which will be implemented through the TAs and consortium. This approach will be conducted and feasibility studies prepared by the TA component.

In addition, along with the above mentioned another concern for TAP was, and still it is, the implementation complexity. Practically, this doctrine does not have as an entity that is clearly responsible to GCF, one that will fully coordinate and control the programme. Although the AEs are the main actors in the implementation of the programme at this stage time, this statement is not supported by a relevant legal design of the implementation process. It is the opinion of TAP that the consortium should be established as an independent entity which should be led by the management of the equity funding proposal. As already mentioned above, the TA proposal and the consortium proposal are prepared independently and, therefore, could not be and has not been independently assessed by TAP.

In the current arrangement, the liaison parties between these two funding proposals are two entities: the AEs and the Equity Funders. The AEs are responsible for the implementation of the "consortium" facilitating cooperation among all AEs and EEs. The "consortium" is a common platform that will be established to facilitate coordination and communication between the AEs and GCF. As reported above, the consortium provides SDF Global with diverse experience through these AEs and EEs.

In response to the inquiry by TAP, the AE provided a document, "Implementation Arrangement", that confirms that these two AEs are fully independent and are responsible to the GCF equity funding proposal.

It is the opinion of TAP that, in order to reduce the implementation complexity and increase the efficiency and effectiveness of the whole programme and SDF Global, the AE for the TA component should be included in the consortium. The consortium will be led by and accountable to the AE of the equity funding proposal. In particular, IUCN should be included in the equity funding proposal as a member of the consortium. This arrangement would provide clear reporting function to the AE of the equity funding proposal (functions other than reporting could also be defined). Reports on the TA component should be submitted to the AE of the equity funding proposal and not directly to GCF. The same applies for the consortium. An exception could be only the first disbursement. Such an implementation arrangement could reduce the complexity of the programme, increase its efficiency and effectiveness; it would not have investors and reduce the risk of SnCF Global facing a lack of bankable projects.


---
**Page 152**
---

## Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel

### (a) Make approval conditional on the revision of the implementation arrangements:
- In particular, IUCN should be included in the equity funding proposal as AE with a clear reporting function to the AoI of the equity funding proposal. Functions other than reporting should not be assigned to IUCN.
- The revised implementation arrangements should be submitted to the AE of the equity funding proposal and should be cleared by this entity for disbursement purposes, while IUCN could be the first disbursement.

### (b) Before the first disbursement:
- S&CF Global should be legally established.
- All EEs should provide operational manuals including:
  - For Pegasus: updated sections on the structure and operation of S&CF Global, and on methodologies for project identification and for the public sector,
  - For IUCN and R2O: methodologies for a market/demand study, sector barrier analysis, establishment of final criteria for selection of subnational projects and for coordination with other EEs.

### (c) For Gold Standard:
- Sectoral tools and methodologies for monitoring, reporting, and third-party certification by priority sectors of S&CF Global.

TAP requests that the Board consider the following points in the event of a positive decision might be more particular than usual due to the uncertainty contained in a "blind pool" first stage selection process. It is critical that the Board has a clear understanding of the programme's operation, TAP agrees it would be critical for the AEs to seek Board consideration of the proposed two AE implementation arrangements prior to undertaking further investment.
